\NatBibNumeric

90C25, 90C06, 90-08
\ORMSCLASSMathematical programming; operations research; game theory
New Accelerated Past-Extragradient Methods with Variance Reduction for Generalized Equations
Quoc Tran-Dinh and Nghia Nguyen-Trung
Abstract
We develop a novel past-extragradient-type algorithmic framework, combining both Nesterov’s acceleration and variance-reduction techniques, to solve a class of generalized equations involving possibly nonmonotone operators in data-driven applications.
Our framework covers a wide class of stochastic variance-reduced schemes, including mini-batching and both unbiased and biased control-variate estimators.
We establish that our method achieves $\mathcal{O}\big(1/k^{2}\big)$ convergence rates in expectation for the squared norm of the residual under Lipschitz continuity and a “co-hypomonotonicity-type” assumption, significantly improving upon non-accelerated counterparts by a factor of $1/k$ .
We also prove faster $o\big(1/k^{2}\big)$ convergence rates, both in expectation and almost surely.
In addition, we show that the sequence of iterates generated by our method almost surely converges to a solution of the underlying problem.
We demonstrate the applicability of our method using general error approximation criteria, covering mini-batch stochastic estimators as well as three well-known control variate estimators: Loopless SVRG, SAGA, and Loopless SARAH.
The resulting three variants attain significantly better oracle complexities than existing methods.
We validate our framework and theoretical results through three numerical examples.
The numerical results illustrate promising performance of our accelerated method over its non-accelerated counterparts.

Past-extragradient method;
Nesterov’s acceleration;

1 Introduction.
The goal of this paper is to develop a novel accelerated past-extragradient-type framework with both unbiased and biased variance reduction techniques to solve the following possibly nonmonotone generalized equation:
${\textrm{Find $x^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}$ such that:~ $0\in\Phi{x}^{\star}$ with $\Phi{x}:=G{x}+Tx$},}\vskip-2.15277pt$
(GE)
where $G:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}$ is a single-valued mapping and $T:\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{R}^{p}$ is a possibly multivalued mapping.
Throughout this paper, we assume that $\mathrm{zer}(\Phi):=\{x^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:0\in\Phi{x}^{\star}\}\neq\emptyset$ , i.e., (GE) has a solution.
Following [95], we refer to (GE) as a composite generalized equation, but it is also known as a composite [non]linear inclusion, see [11].
In particular, if $T=0$ , then (GE) reduces to the following [non]linear equation (also known as a root-finding problem):
${\textrm{Find $x^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}$ such that:~ $Gx^{\star}=0$}.}\vskip-2.15277pt$
(NE)
This equation is equivalent to the following fixed-point problem:
${\textrm{Find $x^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}$ such that $x^{\star}=Fx^{\star}$, where $Fx:=x-\lambda Gx$ for any $\lambda>0$}.}\vskip-2.15277pt$
(FP)
However, under appropriate conditions (e.g., maximal [co-hypo]monotonicity of $T$ ), we can reformulate (GE) equivalently as (NE) using, e.g., a forward-backward splitting, backward-forward splitting, or Tseng’s forward-backward-forward splitting operator, see, e.g., [11, 102].
Under such conditions, we have the following relations: (GE) $\Leftrightarrow$ (NE) $\Leftrightarrow$ (FP).
Problem (GE) looks simple, but it is well-known that (GE) covers the optimality conditions of unconstrained and constrained minimization, minimax, and [mixed] variational inequality problems.
It also comprises Nash equilibrium problems in economics [41].
$\mathrm{1.1.}$ Our problem classes and assumptions.
Before specifying our assumptions, we highlight that we will focus on two classes of (GE) as follows:
1.
Class 1. $G$ is $L$ -Lipschitz continuous and $G+T$ is $\rho$ -co-hypomonotone, i.e., $G$ satisfies Assumption 1 with $\alpha=1$ and $G+T$ satisfies Assumption 1 below with $\rho_{n}\geq 0$ and $\rho_{c}=0$ .
2.
Class 2. $G$ satisfies Assumption 1 with any $\alpha\in[0,1)$ and $G+T$ satisfies both Assumption 1 and Assumption 1 below with $\rho_{n}\geq 0$ and $\rho_{c}>0$ .
In both classes, we assume that $G$ is not directly accessible and that we have access only to an unbiased stochastic oracle $\mathbf{G}$ of $G$ .
More specifically, we impose the following assumptions.
{assumption}
[Unbiased oracle]​​​​​
$G$ is equipped with an unbiased stochastic oracle $\mathbf{G}$ as
${Gx=\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\mathbf{G}(x,\xi)\big],\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{p},}\vskip-2.15277pt$
(1)
where $\xi$ is a random variable on a given probability space $(\Omega,\Sigma,\mathbb{P})$ and $\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[\mathbf{G}(\cdot,\xi)]=\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{G}(\cdot,\xi)\mathbb{P}(d\xi)$ .
Moreover, there exists $\sigma\geq 0$ such that $\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\|\mathbf{G}(x,\xi)-Gx\|^{2}\big]\leq\sigma^{2}$ .
Assumption 1 is widely used in the literature, especially in data-driven optimization applications, see, e.g., [63, 81].
In particular, if we choose $\Omega=[n]:=\{1,\cdots,n\}$ , $\mathbb{P}(\xi=i)=\frac{1}{n}$ for all $i\in\Omega$ , and $\mathbf{G}(x,\xi):=G_{\xi}x$ with $G_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}$ for $i\in[n]$ are single-valued mappings, then $G$ becomes
${Gx=\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\left[\mathbf{G}(x,\xi)\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}(\xi=i)\mathbf{G}(x,i)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}G_{i}x.}\vskip-2.15277pt$
(2)
This is called a finite-sum representation.
It can also be obtained by sample average approximation techniques.
In many applications, $n$ is very large so that evaluating $G$ is expensive or even infeasible.
{assumption}
[Lipschitz continuity-type]
There exist two constants $L\in(0,+\infty)$ and $\alpha\in[0,1]$ such that for all $x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{p}$ , we have
${\begin{array}[]{lcl}\alpha\|Gx-Gy\|^{2}+(1-\alpha)\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\|\mathbf{G}(x,\xi)-\mathbf{G}(y,\xi)\|^{2}\big]\leq L^{2}\|x-y\|^{2}.\end{array}}\vskip-2.15277pt$
(3)
Note that we allow $\alpha=0$ and $\alpha=1$ in Assumption 1.
If $\alpha=1$ , then (3) reduces to $\|Gx-Gy\|\leq L\|x-y\|$ , showing that $G$ is $L$ -Lipschitz continuous.
If $\alpha=0$ , then (3) becomes $\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\|\mathbf{G}(x,\xi)-\mathbf{G}(y,\xi)\|^{2}\big]\leq L^{2}\|x-y\|^{2}$ , showing that $G$ is $L$ -Lipschitz continuous in expectation.
While the former is standard in classical stochastic approximation methods, the latter condition is often used in variance-reduction algorithms with control variate techniques, especially in optimization, see, e.g., [38, 85, 88, 106].
In fact, (3) is a convex combination of these two extreme cases.
Note that $\|Gx-Gy\|^{2}=\|\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\mathbf{G}(x,\xi)-\mathbf{G}(y,\xi)\big]\|^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\|\mathbf{G}(x,\xi)-\mathbf{G}(y,\xi)\|^{2}\big]$ by Jensen’s inequality, (3) implies $\|Gx-Gy\|\leq L\|x-y\|$ for all $x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{p}$ , i.e., $G$ is $L$ -Lipschitz continuous.
In the finite-sum setting (2), our condition (3) reduces to
${\begin{array}[]{lcl}\alpha\|Gx-Gy\|^{2}+\frac{1-\alpha}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|G_{i}x-G_{i}y\|^{2}\leq L^{2}\|x-y\|^{2}.\end{array}}\vskip-2.15277pt$
(4)
If $\alpha=0$ , then $G$ is said to be $L$ -average Lipschitz continuous.
{assumption}
[Weak Minty solution]
There exists a solution $x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)$ of (GE) and a constant $\rho_{*}\geq 0$ such that
${\begin{array}[]{lcl}\langle w,x-x^{\star}\rangle\geq-\rho_{*}\|w\|^{2},\quad\forall(x,w)\in\mathrm{gra}(\Phi).\end{array}}\vskip-2.15277pt$
(5)
This assumption also covers a class of nonmonotone operators $\Phi$ and has been widely used in the literature in recent years, see, e.g., [37, 105] for examples.
It is weaker than the so-called “star-monotonicity” of $\Phi$ (i.e., $\rho_{*}=0$ ) used in some recent works, e.g., [45, 61].
{assumption}
[Co-hypomonotonicity-type]
There exist $\rho_{n}\geq 0$ and $\rho_{c}\geq 0$ such that $\rho_{n}\geq\rho_{c}$ and for all $(x,w_{x}),(y,w_{y})\in\mathrm{gra}(\Phi)$ , we have
${\begin{array}[]{lcl}\langle w_{x}-w_{y},x-y\rangle\geq-\rho_{n}\|w_{x}-w_{y}\|^{2}+\rho_{c}\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\|\mathbf{G}(x,\xi)-\mathbf{G}(y,\xi)\|^{2}\big].\end{array}}\vskip-2.15277pt$
(6)
The condition (6) in Assumption 1 looks technical and nonstandard.
It deserves further discussion.
Note that since we allow $\rho_{c}=0$ , (6) can reduce to
${\begin{array}[]{lcl}\langle w_{x}-w_{y},x-y\rangle\geq-\rho_{n}\|w_{x}-w_{y}\|^{2}.\end{array}}\vskip-2.15277pt$
This relation shows that $\Phi$ is $\rho_{n}$ -co-hypomonotone.
Clearly, Assumption 1 includes ordinary co-hypomonotonicity as a special case and, when $T=0$ , also encompasses average co-coercive operators.
To develop variance-reduction methods via control-variate techniques in Section 4, we will require $\rho_{c}>0$ .
In the finite-sum setting (2), the condition (6) reduces to $\langle w_{x}-w_{y},x-y\rangle\geq-\rho_{n}\|w_{x}-w_{y}\|^{2}+\frac{\rho_{c}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|G_{i}x-G_{i}y\|^{2}$ .
In particular, let us discuss the case when $T=0$ and $\rho_{c}>0$ .
Then, (6) reduces to
${\begin{array}[]{lcl}\langle Gx-Gy,x-y\rangle\geq-\rho_{n}\|Gx-Gy\|^{2}+\rho_{c}\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\|\mathbf{G}(x,\xi)-\mathbf{G}(y,\xi)\|^{2}\big],\quad\forall x,y\in\mathrm{dom}(G).\end{array}}\vskip-2.15277pt$
(7)
We have the following facts corresponding to $G$ in this case.
•
First, if $\mathbf{G}$ is deterministic, i.e., $Gx\equiv\mathbf{G}(x,\xi)$ in (1) or $n=1$ in (2), then (7) reduces to $\langle Gx-Gy,x-y\rangle\geq-\rho\|Gx-Gy\|^{2}$ for $\rho:=\rho_{n}-\rho_{c}$ .
Clearly, if $\rho_{n}<\rho_{c}$ , then $G$ becomes $|\rho|$ -co-coercive, a case excluded by Assumption 1 since we require $\rho_{n}\geq\rho_{c}$ .
If $\rho_{c}=\rho_{n}$ , then $G$ is monotone.
If $\rho_{n}>\rho_{c}$ , then $G$ is $\rho$ -co-hypomonotone (see Subsection 2.1 for the definition of these concepts).
•
Second, since $\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\|\mathbf{G}(x,\xi)-\mathbf{G}(y,\xi)\|^{2}\big]\geq\|\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\mathbf{G}(x,\xi)-\mathbf{G}(y,\xi)\big]\|^{2}$ by Jensen’s inequality, (7) implies that $\langle Gx-Gy,x-y\rangle\geq-\rho\|Gx-Gy\|^{2}$ for $\rho:=\rho_{n}-\rho_{c}$ .
Therefore, if $\rho_{n}<\rho_{c}$ , then (7) is slightly stronger than the $|\rho|$ -co-coercivity of $G$ .
If $\rho_{n}>\rho_{c}$ , then (7) is slightly stronger than the $\rho$ -co-hypomonotonicity of $G$ .
•
Third, since $\rho_{n}\geq 0$ , if $\rho_{c}>0$ , then (7) is considerably weaker than the $\rho_{c}$ -average co-coercivity of $G$ , i.e., $\langle Gx-Gy,x-y\rangle\geq\rho_{c}\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\|\mathbf{G}(x,\xi)-\mathbf{G}(y,\xi)\|^{2}\big]$ .
This $\rho_{c}$ -average-co-coercivity assumption has been widely used in many recent works to develop variance reduction methods for (GE) and its special cases, see, e.g., [24, 33, 100, 102].
Figure 1: Different classes of operators $G$ .
The class “A.4. $(\rho_{c}>0$ )” represents the case $G$ satisfying (7) with $\rho_{c}>0$ .
The class “A.4. $(\rho_{c}=0$ )” covers all co-hypomonotone operators.
In this paper, we do not focus on the case $\rho_{n}<\rho_{c}$ in (7) because $G$ is $(\rho_{c}-\rho_{n})$ -co-coercive, which has been studied in previous works, including our papers [100, 102].
We only consider the case $\rho_{n}\geq\rho_{c}\geq 0$ , which covers a class of monotone and co-hypomonotone operators.
Figure 1 illustrates the class of $\rho$ -co-hypomonotone operators $G$ as well as the class of $G$ satisfying condition (6) when $\rho_{c}>0$ .
The latter intersects with both monotone and nonmonotone operators.
Subsections 2.3 and 2.4 provide many examples of $\Phi$ and $G$ satisfying (6) and (7).
Note that if $T=0$ , then Assumption 1 implies the $\rho$ -co-hypomonotonicity of $G$ with $\rho:=\rho_{n}-\rho_{c}$ .
Thus, Assumption 1 automatically holds with $\rho_{*}=\rho$ .
Alternatively, if $\rho_{c}=0$ , then Assumption 1 also implies the $\rho_{n}$ -co-hypomonotonicity of $\Phi$ , and hence, Assumption 1 also automatically holds with $\rho_{*}=\rho_{n}$ .
In these two cases, Assumption 1 is redundant and can be removed.
$\mathrm{1.2.}$ Motivation and challenges.
Both minimax optimization and variational inequality (VIP) problems are fundamental and powerful tools for modeling two-person games, Nash equilibria, robust optimization [11, 23, 41, 89, 95, 94, 96], and more recently, generative adversarial learning, online learning, reinforcement learning, and distributionally robust optimization [6, 10, 14, 16, 43, 51, 66, 69, 71, 92, 107].
Their optimality conditions can be expressed as a generalized equation (GE).
Yet, unlike classical models, modern applications are data-driven, large-scale, and often involve nonconvex, nonmonotone, and/or nonsmooth structures, limiting the use of deterministic and primal-dual approaches.
Mathematically, these problems are typically formulated as large finite-sums or as stochastic expectations induced by sampled data.
Consequently, contemporary methods face significant challenges, including restrictive assumptions, high computational operation cost, low efficiency, and slow convergence rates.
Hitherto, most methods developed for (GE) and its special cases are deterministic and primarily address monotone problems, see, e.g., [11, 41, 58].
They are inefficient for the finite-sum setting (2) when $n$ is large, and inapplicable to the expectation setting (1), where $G$ is not directly accessible.
Randomized and stochastic methods exist for (GE), but under restrictive assumptions [21, 30, 31, 61, 87].
Many of these still rely on the classical Robbins-Monro approach without acceleration, resulting in slow convergence rates and oracle complexities often ranging from $\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-6}\big)$ to $\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-4}\big)$ , depending on the method and assumptions, where $\epsilon$ is a desired accuracy level.
Recently, several works have applied variance-reduction techniques using control variates, commonly developed in optimization, to (GE), improving oracle complexity over the classical stochastic approximation.
However, these algorithms are non-accelerated with slow convergence rates, typically $\mathcal{O}\big(1/k\big)$ , see, e.g., [1, 2, 33].
While [1, 2] rely on Lipschitz continuity of $G$ and monotonicity of $\Phi$ , others such as [24, 33, 100, 102] assume stronger conditions: co-coercivity and/or strong quasi-monotonicity.
Co-coercivity is strong and hard to verify in practice; it is stronger than monotonicity plus Lipschitz continuity and fails for a bilinear mapping $Fx=[\mathbf{L}v,-\mathbf{L}^{T}u]$ , where $x=(u,v)$ and $\mathbf{L}$ is given.
Moreover, checking co-coercivity of the saddle mapping in minimax optimization applications is non-trivial, even in the convex-concave case.
$\mathrm{1.3.}$ Our goal and approach.
The above shortcomings raise the following research question:
Can we develop a single-loop algorithmic framework capable of achieving faster $\mathcal{O}\big(1/k^{2}\big)$ and $o\big(1/k^{2}\big)$ convergence rates via acceleration, covering a wide range of variants $($ using both unbiased and biased estimators $)$ , attaining improved oracle complexity through variance reduction, and requiring weaker assumptions $($ including a subclass of nonmonotone problems $)$ ?
This paper aims to address this question.
However, we face two main technical challenges.
First, classical Krasnosel’kiǐ-Mann (KM) iteration is not directly applicable under our assumptions, requiring a shift to extragradient-type (EG) schemes.
Nevertheless, EG involves the composite mapping $G(x-\eta Gx)$ , which complicates the design and analysis of stochastic algorithms.
We instead exploit Popov’s past-extragradient method [90] (also closely related to the optimistic gradient scheme) to overcome this limitation.
Second, developing variance-reduction methods with control variate techniques requires a structural assumption on $G$ , often called “average co-coercivity” [24, 33, 100, 102], which excludes certain problem classes, particularly nonmonotone ones.
We identify a new class of problems that includes both monotone and nonmonotone operators.
This class typically satisfies Assumptions 1, 1, and 1.
Unlike previous approaches, our algorithmic development simultaneously combines three main ideas: the past-extragradient method, Nesterov’s acceleration, and unbiased and biased variance-reduction techniques.
While each has been studied extensively, their unified combination to solve (GE) under Assumptions 1-1 remains largely unexplored, especially for nonmonotone problems.
$\mathrm{1.4.}$ Our contributions.
To this end, our contributions consist of the following:
$\mathrm{(a)}$
First, we introduce Assumption 1 to identify a new class of problems in (GE) that goes beyond standard monotonicity and co-coercivity assumptions.
Concrete examples in Subsection 2.4 illustrate the scope and relevance of this assumption, showing that it covers subclasses of both monotone and nonmonotone operators.
$\mathrm{(b)}$
Second, we develop a novel stochastic Nesterov-type accelerated past-extragradient framework to approximate solutions of (GE).
Our framework only requires one stochastic approximation $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ of $Gy^{k}$ per iteration, evaluated at the iterate $y^{k}$ .
We introduce a general error approximation condition connecting $Gy^{k}$ to its (unbiased or biased) stochastic variance-reduced estimator $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ , constructed via the stochastic oracle $\mathbf{G}(\cdot,\xi)$ , which forms the basis of our convergence analysis.
Alternatively, we also propose a class of control-variate-based estimators of $G$ for our framework, covering both unbiased and biased instances, including SVRG [54], SAGA [34], and SARAH [85].
$\mathrm{(c)}$
Third, we establish $\mathcal{O}\big(1/k^{2}\big)$ convergence rates in expectation for $\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]$ using either the proposed error approximation criterion or our class of variance-reduced estimators, where $x^{k}$ is the iterate and $v^{k}\in Tx^{k}$ .
Our rate is significantly better than non-accelerated counterparts, e.g., [1, 2, 101], by a factor of $1/k$ .
We further prove $o\big(1/k^{2}\big)$ convergence of $\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}$ both in expectation and almost surely, and show that the iterate sequences $\{x^{k}\}$ , $\{y^{k}\}$ , and $\{z^{k}\}$ converge almost surely to a $\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)$ -valued random variable $x^{\star}$ , a solution of (GE).
$\mathrm{(d)}$
Finally, we demonstrate our framework with three common estimators: Loopless SVRG, SAGA, and Loopless SARAH, and analyze their oracle complexities for the finite-sum case (2).
For Loopless SVRG and SAGA, we obtain $\mathcal{O}\big(n\log(n)+n^{2/3}\epsilon^{-1}\big)$ , while for Loopless SARAH we achieve $\mathcal{O}\big(n\log(n)+n^{1/2}\epsilon^{-1}\big)$ , where $\epsilon>0$ is the target accuracy.
We believe that our framework is simple to implement, as it is single-loop and uses elementary operations.
Moreover, it includes a broad class of variance-reduced and accelerated algorithms, covering mini-batch, unbiased, and biased estimators, a feature that is rarely seen in optimization.
Unlike most existing methods limited to the monotone setting or special cases of (GE), our approach can handle a class of nonmonotone generalized equations.
Its convergence guarantees are notable, covering $\mathcal{O}\big(1/k^{2}\big)$ and $o\big(1/k^{2}\big)$ convergence rates, and almost sure convergence of iterates.
The $o\big(1/k^{2}\big)$ rates and almost-sure convergence of the iterates have received limited attention, even in variance-reduction methods for convex optimization.
$\mathrm{1.5.}$ Related work and comparison.
Problem (GE) is classical, with its theory and numerical methods extensively studied over decades, as presented in monographs such as [11, 41, 77, 95] and references therein.
In this paper, we focus on a different class of (GE) and develop novel stochastic single-loop methods with acceleration and variance reduction.
Accordingly, we restrict our review and comparison to works along this direction.

$\mathrm{(a)}$ From non-accelerated to accelerated methods.
The key advantage of accelerated methods over non-accelerated ones is their faster convergence rates: $\mathcal{O}\big(1/k^{2}\big)$ vs. $\mathcal{O}\big(1/k\big)$ .
Nesterov first introduced acceleration for smooth convex optimization [83], later extended to other problems and monotone inclusions, including (GE) [7, 9]. This line of research has recently gained strong attention, especially in deterministic settings [20, 22, 57, 72, 103, 104].
As an alternative, Halpern’s fixed-point iteration [49] also achieves accelerated $\mathcal{O}\big(1/k^{2}\big)$ rates on the squared norm of residual for (GE) under co-coercivity [36, 68, 97], improving on the classical $\mathcal{O}\big(1/k\big)$ rate of the Krasnosel’kiǐ-Mann (KM) method and its variants.
These advances have driven extensive work in operator theory, mostly in deterministic settings [26, 27, 65, 103, 109]. Though Nesterov- and Halpern-type accelerations were developed independently, their connection was later established in [99] and also in [86].
In this paper, we build on Nesterov’s acceleration to design stochastic variance-reduction algorithms for (GE) under Assumptions 1–1 and establish their rigorous convergence guarantees.
$\mathrm{(b)}$ From convex optimization to generalized equations.
In convex optimization, the $L$ -smoothness of the objective function is equivalent to its gradient co-coercivity [11, 84], making smooth convex minimization problems special cases of (NE) with $G$ being co-coercive.
By contrast, smooth convex-concave minimax problems often lack this property (see, e.g., bilinear game models [41]), highlighting a key distinction between optimization and generalized equations (GE). This shift complicates algorithm design and convergence analysis, as many standard analysis tools in convex optimization no longer apply to (GE) [9, 57, 99].
One technical challenge is the absence of an objective function in (GE), complicating the construction of a suitable Lyapunov function that underpins convergence guarantees in accelerated methods.
In addition, unlike optimization, developing accelerated randomized and variance-reduced methods for data-driven (GE) remains challenging but crucial.
Beyond co-coercivity, extragradient (EG) and its variants [59, 90, 32] are prominent, but their stochastic extensions face difficulties: dependence on intermediate updates [110] and the breakdown of gap function-based analysis under nonmonotonicity [1, 2].
Dual averaging and mirror descent [82, 61] are similarly restricted to monotone problems.
In this work, we extend Popov’s past-extragradient variant to the stochastic setting, addressing cases with biased estimators.
$\mathrm{(c)}$ Comparison with existing stochastic methods.
Stochastic methods for (GE) often follow the Robbins-Monro stochastic approximation framework, but converge slowly, typically at an $\mathcal{O}\big(1/k\big)$ rate with inefficient oracle complexity, see, e.g., [19, 28, 31, 52, 55, 56, 60].
Recent work improves this limitation via variance reduction techniques, e.g., adaptive or growing mini-batches [21, 87].
Alternative approaches such as dual averaging and mirror descent [29, 61] extend to VIPs but require monotonicity, limiting applicability.
Other advances include stochastic splitting and mirror-prox methods [15, 44, 70].
In contrast, our work employs accelerated methods with a broad class of unbiased and biased variance-reduced estimators, including several well-known instances.
$\mathrm{(d)}$ Comparison with variance-reduction methods using control variate techniques.
In the co-coercive setting, several variance-reduced methods using control variates have been proposed [24, 33, 100, 102], exploiting co-coercivity for faster rates and improved oracle complexity. Table 1 summarizes recent works by assumptions, estimators, and guarantees.
Beyond co-coercivity, EG variants, often based on Popov’s scheme [90], have been studied for monotone VIPs.
Examples include SVRG-based methods in [1, 2, 110], which use gap functions for convergence analysis and rely on bounded-variance or unbiased estimators.
Our approach retains a simple single-loop structure, similar to the methods in [1, 2, 24, 33], but it further (i) incorporates acceleration, (ii) applies to “co-hypomonotone-type” operators, (iii) accommodates both unbiased and biased estimators under general error-bound conditions, (iv) establishes stronger convergence guarantees, including $o\big(1/k^{2}\big)$ rates in expectation and almost surely, as well as almost sure convergence of the iterates to a solution, and (v) achieves improved oracle complexity under weaker assumptions.
Unlike [1, 110], our method also provides residual convergence guarantees with better oracle complexity in the finite-sum setting.
$\mathrm{1.6.}$ Paper organization.
The remainder of this paper is organized as follows.
Section 2 reviews the necessary background and concepts, and presents examples of $\Phi$ and $G$ satisfying Assumption 1.
Section 3 develops a unified accelerated past-extragradient-type framework with variance reduction to solve (GE) for the expectation setting (1) under Assumption 1, Assumption 1 with $\alpha=1$ , and Assumption 1 with $\rho_{c}=0$ , establishing convergence under a general error bound condition.
Section 4 introduces an alternative variant using control-variate-based variance reduction to handle both (1) and (2), with similar convergence guarantees.
Section 5 provides a complete convergence analysis of the proposed methods.
Section 6 presents three numerical examples to validate our theoretical results and benchmark against state-of-the-art methods.
Preliminary results and technical proofs are deferred to the appendix.
2 Background, Preliminaries, and Examples.
We recall necessary notation and concepts used in this paper, and briefly describe Popov’s method.
Finally, we provide different examples to illustrate Assumption 1.
2.1 Notation and concepts.
First, we recall necessary notation and concepts.
Basic concepts.
We work with a finite-dimensional space $\mathbb{R}^{p}$ equipped with the standard inner product $\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle$ and norm $\|\cdot\|$ .
For a function $f:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}$ , $\nabla{f}$ denotes the gradient of $f$ , $\partial{f}$ denotes the subdifferential of $f$ , and $\mathrm{prox}_{f}$ is the proximal operator of $f$ .
For a given random vector $\mathbf{u}_{\xi}$ in $\mathbb{R}^{p}$ and $v\in\mathbb{R}^{p}$ , we define $u:=\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[\mathbf{u}_{\xi}]$ and $|\!\|u+v|\!\|^{2}:=\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[\|\mathbf{u}_{\xi}+v\|^{2}]$ .
By Jensen’s inequality, we also have $|\!\|u+v|\!\|^{2}\geq\|u+v\|^{2}$ .
For a given symmetric matrix $\mathbf{Q}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}$ , $\mathbf{Q}\succeq 0$ (resp., $\mathbf{Q}\succ 0$ ) means that $\mathbf{Q}$ is positive semidefinite (resp., positive definite).
For two symmetric matrices $\mathbf{P}$ and $\mathbf{Q}$ , $\mathbf{P}\succeq\mathbf{Q}$ means $\mathbf{P}-\mathbf{Q}\succeq 0$ .
For given functions $g(t)$ and $h(t)$ , we say that $g(t)=\mathcal{O}\big(h(t)\big)$ if there exist $M>0$ and $t_{0}\geq 0$ such that $g(t)\leq Mh(t)$ for $t\geq t_{0}$ .
We use $\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(g(t))$ to hide a poly-log factor of $g(t)$ .
Filtration and expectations.
Let $\mathcal{F}_{k}$ denote the $\sigma$ -algebra generated by all the randomness of our algorithm up to iteration $k$ , including the iterates $x^{0},\cdots,x^{k}$ .
We also denote by $\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\cdot\big]:=\mathbb{E}\big[\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}\big]$ the conditional expectation conditioned on $\mathcal{F}_{k}$ , and by $\mathbb{E}\big[\cdot\big]$ the full expectation.
For a random variable $\xi$ , we denote by $\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\cdot\big]$ the expectation w.r.t. $\xi$ .
Lipschitz continuity.
For a single-valued mapping $G:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}$ , we say that $G$ is $L$ -Lipschitz continuous if there exists $L\geq 0$ such that $\|Gx-Gy\|\leq L\|x-y\|$ for all $x,y\in\mathrm{dom}(G)$ .
If $L<1$ , then $G$ is contractive, and if $L=1$ , then $G$ is nonexpansive.
Co-monotonicity, monotonicity, and co-coercivity.
For a single-valued or multivalued mapping $G:\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{R}^{p}$ , we denote by $\mathrm{dom}(G):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:Gx\neq\emptyset\}$ the domain of $G$ and by $\mathrm{gra}(G):=\{(x,u)\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}:u\in Gx\}$ the graph of $G$ .
If $G$ is differentiable at $x$ , then $G^{\prime}(x)$ denotes its derivative at $x$ .
We say that $G$ has a closed graph $\mathrm{gra}(G)$ if for any sequence $\{(x^{k},u^{k})\}$ such that $(x^{k},u^{k})\in\mathrm{gra}(G)$ and $(x^{k},u^{k})\to(x,u)$ as $k\to\infty$ , then $(x,u)\in\mathrm{gra}(G)$ .
For further details on these concepts and properties, we refer the reader to [11].
Definition 2.1
Let $G:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to 2^{\mathbb{R}^{p}}$ be a given single-valued or multivalued mapping.
•
We say that $G$ is monotone if for all $(x,u),(y,v)\in\mathrm{gra}(G)$ , we have $\langle u-v,x-y\rangle\geq 0$ .
•
We say that $G$ is $\rho$ -co-monotone if there exists $\rho\in\mathbb{R}$ such that $\langle u-v,x-y\rangle\geq\rho\|u-v\|^{2}$ for all $(x,u),(y,v)\in\mathrm{gra}(G)$ .
Clearly, if $\rho=0$ , then $G$ reduces to a monotone mapping.
See [12] for the definitions and further properties.
$\diamond$
If $\langle u-v,x-y\rangle\geq\rho\|u-v\|^{2}$ for $\rho<0$ , then we say that $G$ is $|\rho|$ -co-hypomonotone.
A $|\rho|$ -co-hypomonotone mapping can be nonmonotone (see examples, e.g., in [102]).
$\diamond$
If $\langle u-v,x-y\rangle\geq\rho\|u-v\|^{2}$ for $\rho>0$ , then we say that $G$ is $\rho$ -co-coercive.
Moreover, if $G$ is $\rho$ -co-coercive, then it is also monotone and $L$ -Lipschitz continuous with $L:=\frac{1}{\rho}$ .
If $f$ is a convex and $L$ -smooth (i.e., $\nabla{f}$ is $L$ -Lipschitz continuous) function, then $\nabla{f}$ is $\frac{1}{L}$ -co-coercive.
The $\rho$ -co-coercivity of a mapping $G$ is also equivalent to the nonexpansiveness of $F=\mathbb{I}-2\rho G$ in the fixed-point problem (FP), see [11].
All the concepts defined above are global.
If $G$ satisfies any of the above properties for all $(x,u),(y,v)\in\mathrm{gra}(G)\cap\mathcal{W}$ , where $\mathcal{W}$ is a neighborhood of $(\bar{x},\bar{u})$ in $\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}$ , then we say that $G$ has such a local property around $(\bar{x},\bar{u})\in\mathrm{gra}(G)$ .
2.2 The past-extragradient method.
A classical method for solving (GE) in the variational inequality setting $T=\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{X}}$ (the normal cone of $\mathcal{X}$ ), where $\mathcal{X}$ is nonempty, closed, and convex and $G$ is monotone and Lipschitz continuous, is Korpelevich’s extragradient method, introduced by Korpelevich in [59] in 1976 (also by Antipin in [4] (1976)).
This method has inspired various variants, among which the past-extragradient method, a notable variant proposed by Popov in [90], is particularly well-known.
When applied to solving (GE), this variant is equivalent to the optimistic gradient method studied in [32, 76, 79].
This method starts from $x^{0}\in\mathrm{dom}(\Phi)$ , chooses $y^{-1}:=x^{0}$ , and at each iteration $k\geq 0$ , updates
$\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}y^{k}&:=&J_{\eta T}(x^{k}-\eta Gy^{k-1}),\\
x^{k+1}&:=&J_{\eta T}(x^{k}-\eta Gy^{k}),\end{array}\right.$
(PEG)
where $\eta>0$ is a given stepsize.
This scheme has been well studied in the literature, including [75, 90, 105], mostly in the deterministic setting.
It also has close connections to other methods such as the projected reflected gradient scheme [73], the forward-reflected-backward splitting algorithm [75], and the golden-ratio method [74].
As mentioned, when $T=0$ , it is also equivalent to the optimistic gradient method [32, 76, 79].
Inspired by (PEG), we develop an accelerated algorithmic framework with variance reduction in Section 3 to solve (GE) under Assumptions 1-1.
2.3 Examples of co-hypomonotone operators.
We provide here a number of mappings that are co-hypomonotone, but not necessarily monotone.
$\mathrm{(a)}$ Example 1.
As shown in [102], given a $p\times p$ symmetric and invertible matrix $\mathbf{G}$ and a vector $\mathbf{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}$ , an affine mapping $Gx:=\mathbf{G}x+\mathbf{g}$ is $\rho$ -co-monotone with $\rho:=\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{G}^{-1})$ .
If $\rho:=\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{G}^{-1})<0$ , then $G$ is nonmonotone, but $|\rho|$ -co-hypomonotone.
More generally, for an arbitrary matrix $\mathbf{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}$ , the affine mapping $Gx:=\mathbf{G}x+\mathbf{g}$ is $\rho$ -co-hypomonotone for some $\rho\geq 0$ if and only if $\mathbf{G}+\mathbf{G}^{\top}+2\rho\mathbf{G}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\succeq 0$ .
As shown in Example 4 below, the subdifferentials of certain nonconvex regularizers are not linear but piecewise linear, and they are also locally $\rho$ -co-hypomonotone.
$\mathrm{(b)}$ Example 2.
As shown in [40, Proposition 4.17], consider a twice continuously differentiable function $\Psi:\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{p_{2}}\to\mathbb{R}$ and its associated saddle mapping $\Phi(u,v):=[\nabla_{u}\Psi(u,v),-\nabla_{v}\Psi(u,v)]$ .
If $\Psi$ satisfies the $\alpha$ -interaction-dominance condition with $\alpha\geq 0$ and parameter $\rho>0$ , then $\Phi$ is $\rho$ -co-hypomonotone.
The interaction-dominance condition was introduced in [48] for studying smooth nonconvex-nonconcave minimax problems.
Recently, [40] generalized co-hypomonotonicity to semi-monotonicity, which encompasses both hypomonotonicity and co-hypomonotonicity while covering broader parameter regimes.
$\mathrm{(c)}$ Example 3.
Let $A:\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{R}^{p}$ be a monotone operator and $\rho>0$ be given.
Then, the mapping $Tx:=-\rho^{-1}x-Ax$ is $\rho$ -co-hypomonotone and, in general, nonmonotone (see Appendix A.2).
$\mathrm{(d)}$ Example 4.
As shown in [102], consider the [generalized] subdifferential $Tx:=\partial{\mathrm{SCAD}_{a}}(x)$ of the SCAD (smoothly clipped absolute deviation) regularizer from [42] with $a=3.7$ , which is widely used in statistics.
The operator $T$ is not globally $\rho$ -co-hypomonotone.
However, on each smooth concave region of $\mathrm{SCAD}_{a}$ , it is $\rho$ -co-hypomonotone, where $\rho:=a-1$ .
More precisely, $\langle u-v,x-y\rangle=-(a-1)\|u-v\|^{2}$ for all $(x,u),(y,v)\in\mathrm{gra}(T)$ belonging to the same concave region.
Similar local co-hypomonotonicity properties hold for several other well-known nonconvex regularizers, such as MCP (minimax concave penalty) and the log-sum penalty.
2.4 Examples of co-hypomonotone operators satisfying Assumption 1.
The Lipschitz continuity-type condition (3) in Assumption 1 is common, especially when $\alpha=0$ or $\alpha=1$ .
Let us provide concrete examples that satisfy our “co-hypomonotonicity-type” condition (6) in Assumption 1 and condition (7) (i.e., $T=0$ ).
$\mathrm{(a)}$ Example 1 (Concrete linear mappings).
We first consider a concrete linear example.
Consider the following three square matrices $\mathbf{G}_{1}$ , $\mathbf{G}_{2}$ , and $\hat{\mathbf{G}}$ and two vectors $\mathbf{g}_{1}$ and $\mathbf{g}_{2}$ :
$\mathbf{G}_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}-2&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix},\quad\mathbf{G}_{2}=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},\quad\hat{\mathbf{G}}=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&1/2\end{bmatrix},\quad\mathbf{g}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathbf{g}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}-1\\
1\end{pmatrix}.$
(8)
We define three mappings $G_{1}x:=\mathbf{G}_{1}x+\mathbf{g}_{1}$ , $G_{2}x:=\mathbf{G}_{2}x+\mathbf{g}_{2}$ , and $Tx:=\hat{\mathbf{G}}x$ .
Then, we have $Gx:=\frac{1}{2}(G_{1}x+G_{2}x)$ and $\Phi{x}:=Gx+Tx$ .
Since $\mathbf{\Phi}:=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{G}_{1}+\mathbf{G}_{2})+\hat{\mathbf{G}}=\begin{bmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}$ is not positive semidefinite, $\Phi$ is nonmonotone.
Now, for any vector $u\in\mathbb{R}^{2}$ , we can easily check that for some $\rho_{n}\geq\rho_{c}>0$ , we have
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}u^{\top}\mathbf{\Phi}u\geq-\rho_{n}\|\mathbf{\Phi}u\|^{2}+\frac{\rho_{c}}{2}\big(\|\mathbf{G}_{1}u\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{G}_{2}u\|^{2}\big).\end{array}$
(9)
Since $\mathbf{\Phi}$ is symmetric, the condition (9) is equivalent to
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbf{\Phi}+\rho_{n}\mathbf{\Phi}^{\top}\mathbf{\Phi}-\frac{\rho_{c}}{2}\big(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{1}+\mathbf{G}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{2}\big)\succeq 0.\end{array}$
By a direct calculation, there exist $\rho_{n}:=2\epsilon+1$ and $\rho_{c}:=\epsilon>0$ for any $\epsilon>0$ such that this inequality holds.
Therefore, with these given $\rho_{n}$ and $\rho_{c}$ , for all $x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}$ , we have
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\langle\Phi{x}-{\Phi}y,x-y\rangle\geq-\rho_{n}\|\Phi{x}-\Phi{y}\|^{2}+\frac{\rho_{c}}{2}\big(\|G_{1}x-G_{1}y\|^{2}+\|G_{2}x-G_{2}y\|^{2}\big).\end{array}$
Overall, we have shown that the mappings $G$ and $T$ satisfy Assumption 1.
$\mathrm{(b)}$ Example 2 (A class of (GE) studied in [24, 100, 102]).
In [24, 100, 102], the authors studied a class of (GE) in which $G$ is $\frac{1}{L}$ -co-coercive in expectation, i.e., for all $x,y\in\mathrm{dom}(G)$ , there exists $L>0$ such that
$\begin{array}[]{l}\langle Gx-Gy,x-y\rangle=\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\langle\mathbf{G}(x,\xi)-\mathbf{G}(y,\xi),x-y\rangle\big]\geq\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\|\mathbf{G}(x,\xi)-\mathbf{G}(y,\xi)\|^{2}\big],\end{array}$
and $T$ is maximally $\rho$ -co-hypomonotone [100, 102] or monotone [24].
As shown in Appendix A.3, one can check that $\Phi:=G+T$ satisfies (6) in Assumption 1 with $\rho_{n}:=\frac{(1+\tau)\rho}{\tau}\geq 0$ and $0\leq\rho_{c}\leq\frac{1}{L}-(1+\tau)\rho$ for any $\tau>0$ .
Here, if $0\leq L\rho\leq\frac{1}{1+\tau}<1$ , then $\rho_{c}\geq 0$ .
$\mathrm{(c)}$ Example 3 (General linear mappings).
Let us consider the following linear mappings:
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}G_{i}x:=\mathbf{G}_{i}x+\mathbf{g}_{i}\quad(i\in[n])\quad\textrm{and}\quad Gx:=\mathbf{G}x+\mathbf{g}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}G_{i}x,\end{array}$
(10)
where $\mathbf{G}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}$ and $\mathbf{g}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}$ for $i\in[n]$ are given, $\mathbf{G}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{G}_{i}$ , and $\mathbf{g}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{g}_{i}$ .
Suppose that there exist $\rho_{n}>0$ and $\rho_{c}>0$ such that
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{G}+\mathbf{G}^{\top}\big)+\rho_{n}\mathbf{G}^{\top}\mathbf{G}-\frac{\rho_{c}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{G}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{i}\succeq 0.\end{array}$
(11)
Then, one can easily show that $G$ satisfies the condition (7), a special case of the condition (6) in Assumption 1 when $T=0$ .
If $\lambda_{\min}(\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{G}+\mathbf{G}^{\top}))<0$ , then $G$ is nonmonotone.
Additionally, if we assume that $\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{G}^{\top}\mathbf{G})>0$ (i.e., $\mathbf{G}$ is nonsingular), then we can choose
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\rho_{n}\geq-\frac{\lambda_{\min}(\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{G}+\mathbf{G}^{\top}))}{\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{G}^{\top}\mathbf{G})}>0\quad\textrm{and}\quad 0\leq\rho_{c}\leq\frac{n\left[\lambda_{\min}(\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{G}+\mathbf{G}^{\top}))+\rho_{n}\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{G}^{\top}\mathbf{G})\right]}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{G}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{i})}.\end{array}$
(12)
In this case, $\rho_{n}$ and $\rho_{c}$ chosen as in (12) satisfy (11).
Consequently, the condition (7) (a special case of Assumption 1 when $T=0$ ) is satisfied.
$\mathrm{(d)}$ Example 4 (Bounded differential operator diversity).
For given $n$ operators $G_{i}$ , we define their difference diversity ratio:
$R(x,y):=\frac{n\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|G_{i}x-G_{i}y\|^{2}}{\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(G_{i}x-G_{i}y)\|^{2}},\quad\forall x,y\in\mathrm{dom}(G),\ Gx\neq Gy.$
(13)
This concept is similar to the differential gradient diversity concept in [108] often used in federated learning and distributed optimization.
Here, we extend it to operators.
Since $\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(G_{i}x-G_{i}y)\|^{2}\leq n\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|G_{i}x-G_{i}y\|^{2}$ by Jensen’s inequality, we have $R(x,y)\geq 1$ for all $x,y\in\mathrm{dom}(G)$ .
Next, we assume that $R(x,y)$ is upper bounded for all $x,y\in\mathrm{dom}(G)$ , i.e., there exists $M\geq 1$ such that $1\leq R(x,y)\leq M$ for all $x,y\in\mathrm{dom}(G)$ .
Now, suppose that $G$ is also $\rho$ -co-hypomonotone with $\rho>0$ .
Then it satisfies the condition (7) in Assumption 1 with $\rho_{n}>\rho$ and $\rho_{c}:=\frac{\rho_{n}-\rho}{M}>0$ (see Appendix A.3).
$\mathrm{(e)}$ Example 5 (Bounded derivatives).
Suppose that $\mathrm{dom}(G_{i})$ is convex and $G_{i}$ is continuously differentiable for all $i\in[n]$ .
Let $\bar{\sigma}_{i}:=\sup_{x\in\mathrm{dom}(G_{i})}\sigma_{\max}(G_{i}^{\prime}(x))$ be the upper bound of the largest singular value of $G_{i}^{\prime}(x)$ for $i\in[n]$ and $\underline{\sigma}>0$ be the smallest constant such that $\|Gx-Gy\|\geq\underline{\sigma}\|x-y\|$ for all $x,y\in\mathrm{dom}(G)$ .
Denote $\bar{\sigma}^{2}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{\sigma}_{i}^{2}$ .
Suppose that $0<\underline{\sigma}\leq\bar{\sigma}<+\infty$ .
Then, if $G$ is additionally $\rho$ -co-hypomonotone with $\rho>0$ , then it satisfies the condition (7) in Assumption 1 with $\rho_{n}>\rho$ and $\rho_{c}:=\frac{(\rho_{n}-\rho)\underline{\sigma}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}>0$ (see Appendix A.3).
3 Variance Reduced Fast Optimistic Gradient Method for the General Case.
In this section, we exploit three main ideas: Popov’s past-EG scheme, Nesterov’s acceleration, and variance reduction techniques to develop a novel algorithmic framework for solving a class of (GE) under the Lipschitz continuity of $G$ and the $\rho_{n}$ -co-hypomonotonicity of $\Phi$ .
That is: we solve a class of (GE) covered by Assumption 1 with $\alpha=1$ and Assumption 1 with $\rho_{n}\geq 0$ and $\rho_{c}=0$ .
3.1 The proposed algorithm.
The following variant of Nesterov’s accelerated method from [13, 83] for minimizing the sum $\phi:=f+g$ of a convex and $L$ -smooth function $f$ and a possibly nonsmooth and convex function $g$ can be expressed as follows:
$\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hat{x}^{k}&:=&\frac{t_{k}-s}{t_{k}}x^{k}+\frac{s}{t_{k}}z^{k},\\
x^{k+1}&:=&\hat{x}^{k}-\eta(\nabla{f}(\hat{x}^{k})+v^{k+1}),\\
z^{k+1}&:=&z^{k}-\frac{\eta t_{k}}{s}(\nabla{f}(\hat{x}^{k})+v^{k+1}),\end{array}\right.\vskip-2.15277pt$
(NAG)
where $v^{k+1}\in\partial{g}(x^{k+1})$ , $0<\eta\leq\frac{1}{L}$ is a given stepsize, and $t_{k}=k+s+1$ for some $s>1$ .
Here, we have used the identity $x^{k+1}=\mathrm{prox}_{\eta g}(x^{k+1}+\eta v^{k+1})$ in the second line.
This algorithm is known to achieve an $\mathcal{O}\big(1/k^{2}\big)$ convergence rate on the objective residual $\phi(x^{k})-\phi(x^{\star})$ , significantly improving over the $\mathcal{O}\big(1/k\big)$ rate in the standard proximal gradient method.
Under an appropriate choice of $s$ , (NAG) can achieve up to an $o\big(1/k^{2}\big)$ rate [8].
Main idea.
Our idea is to exploit this (NAG) scheme and combine it with Popov’s past-EG mechanism to develop an accelerated past-extragradient scheme for (GE).
However, since we can only access an unbiased stochastic oracle $\mathbf{G}$ of $G$ , we will approximate the value $Gy^{k}$ by a stochastic variance-reduced estimator $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ constructed from $\mathbf{G}(y^{k},\xi)$ .
This combination leads to the following new Variance-reduced Accelerated Past ExtraGradient method, which we call VAPEG.
The proposed method.
Given an initial point $x^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}$ , we set $z^{0}=y^{-1}:=x^{0}$ , $\widetilde{G}y^{-1}=\widetilde{G}y^{-1}:=\widetilde{G}x^{0}$ , and at each iteration $k\geq 0$ , we construct a stochastic estimator $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ of $Gy^{k}$ , and then update
$\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hat{x}^{k}&:=&\frac{t_{k}-s}{t_{k}}x^{k}+\frac{s}{t_{k}}z^{k},\\
y^{k}&:=&\hat{x}^{k}-(\eta-\beta_{k})(\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}+v^{k}),\\
x^{k+1}&:=&\hat{x}^{k}-\eta(\widetilde{G}y^{k}+v^{k+1})+\beta_{k}(\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}+v^{k}),\\
z^{k+1}&:=&z^{k}-\frac{\gamma_{k}}{s}(\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}+v^{k}),\end{array}\right.$
(VAPEG)
where $v^{k}\in Tx^{k}$ , $t_{k}\geq s>1$ , $\eta>0$ , $\beta_{k}\geq 0$ , $\gamma_{k}>0$ are given parameters, determined later.
There are two key components of (VAPEG) that require further discussion.
•
First, we will clearly characterize the conditions on the estimator $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ of $Gy^{k}$ to guarantee the convergence of (VAPEG).
We will also give concrete examples of $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ .
•
Second, we will specify the choice of parameters $t_{k}$ , $s$ , $\eta$ , $\beta_{k}$ , and $\gamma_{k}$ in our convergence analysis to achieve better convergence than its non-accelerated counterparts.
The scheme (VAPEG) is sufficiently general to cover the following special cases.
$\mathrm{(a)}$
If $\widetilde{G}y^{k}:=Gy^{k}$ and $\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}:=Gx^{k}$ , then we obtain a deterministic fast extragradient method for solving (GE).
It is similar to the one in [104, 111], but the last step is different.
$\mathrm{(b)}$
If $\widetilde{G}y^{k}:=Gy^{k}$ and $\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}:=Gy^{k-1}$ , then we get a deterministic accelerated past-extragradient or fast optimistic gradient (OG) method for solving (GE).
This deterministic variant is also new under Assumptions 1-1, and also different from [104].
Comparison with (PEG) and (NAG).
In fact, (VAPEG) mimics Popov’s past-extragradient scheme, Nesterov’s acceleration, and variance reduction techniques to create such a new algorithm.
Formally, if $T=0$ , $\widetilde{G}y^{k}=Gy^{k}$ , $\beta_{k}=0$ , and $s=0$ , then this scheme reduces to (PEG) discussed in Section 2.
At the same time, it incorporates an intermediate step $\hat{x}^{k}=\frac{t_{k}-s}{t_{k}}x^{k}+\frac{s}{t_{k}}z^{k}$ and uses the varying parameters $t_{k}$ and $\beta_{k}$ , similarly to Nesterov’s accelerated method (NAG) in convex optimization.
However, the first fundamental difference between (NAG) and (VAPEG) is the use of the stepsize $\frac{\gamma_{k}}{s}$ instead of $\frac{\eta t_{k}}{s}$ in the last line of (VAPEG).
The second one is the search direction $\eta\widetilde{G}y^{k}-\beta_{k}\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}$ in (VAPEG) combining two consecutive approximations $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ and $\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}$ of $G$ , which acts like $\eta\nabla{f}(\hat{x}^{k})$ in (NAG) for smooth and convex optimization.
The implementable version.
Since $v^{k+1}$ is on the right-hand side of the third line of (VAPEG), we can use the resolvent $J_{\eta T}$ of $\eta T$ to rewrite it equivalently to
$\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hat{x}^{k}&:=&\frac{s}{t_{k}}z^{k}+\frac{t_{k}-s}{t_{k}}x^{k},\\
y^{k}&:=&\hat{x}^{k}-(\eta-\beta_{k})(\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}+v^{k}),\\
x^{k+1}&:=&J_{\eta T}\big(\hat{x}^{k}-\eta\widetilde{G}y^{k}+\beta_{k}(\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}+v^{k})\big),\\
z^{k+1}&:=&z^{k}-\frac{\gamma_{k}}{s}(\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}+v^{k}),\\
v^{k+1}&:=&\frac{1}{\eta}\big(\hat{x}^{k}-x^{k+1}+\beta_{k}(\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}+v^{k})\big)-\widetilde{G}y^{k}.\end{array}\right.$
(14)
Clearly, each iteration of (14) requires one construction of the estimator $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ and one evaluation of $J_{\eta T}$ .
Note that, for our theoretical analysis, we require the exact evaluation of the resolvent $J_{\eta T}$ .
This requirement is satisfied in many cases where $T$ has a “simple form,” such as the subdifferential of a proximally tractable function (e.g., $\|\cdot\|_{1}$ , coordinate-wise separable functions, or the indicator function of a simple convex set).
In practice, however, the resolvent $J_{\eta T}$ can also be evaluated approximately to a prescribed accuracy, thereby broadening the applicability of our method (14), see, e.g., [94].
3.2 Main result 1: Convergence properties of VAPEG.
There are different strategies to approximate $Gy^{k}$ by $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ .
For instance, one can use increasing mini-batch stochastic approximation strategies as in [21, 87] or control variate techniques as in [34, 54, 85].
In what follows, we do not specify how to approximate $Gy^{k}$ , but introduce a general recursive bound for $\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|\widetilde{G}y^{k}-Gy^{k}\|^{2}\big]$ .
3.2.1 Error approximation condition between $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ and $Gy^{k}$ .
We assume that $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ is constructed by accessing the unbiased stochastic oracle $\mathbf{G}(y^{k},\xi)$ of $Gy^{k}$ adapted to the filtration $\{\mathcal{F}_{k}\}$ such that for $k\geq 0$ , there exist two constants $\kappa\in(0,1]$ and $\Theta\geq 0$ , a nonnegative sequence $\{\delta_{k}\}$ , and a sequence of nonnegative random variables $\{\Delta_{k}\}$ such that the following condition holds almost surely for the error term $e^{k}:=\widetilde{G}y^{k}-Gy^{k}$ :
${\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|e^{k}\|^{2}\big]&\leq&\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\Delta_{k}\big],\\
\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\Delta_{k}\big]&\leq&(1-\kappa)\Delta_{k-1}+\Theta L^{2}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}+\frac{\delta_{k}}{t_{k}^{2}},\end{array}\right.}\vskip-2.15277pt$
(15)
where $\{(x^{k},y^{k})\}$ and $\{t_{k}\}$ are updated by our scheme (VAPEG).
The condition (15) is rather general.
Indeed, if $\kappa=1$ and $\Theta=0$ , then it reduces to $\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|e^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\frac{\delta_{k}}{t_{k}^{2}}$ .
If $\kappa=1$ and $\delta_{k}=0$ , then it becomes $\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|e^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\Theta L^{2}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}$ , which can be viewed as an adaptive error bound condition.
One way to choose $\delta_{k}$ is $\delta_{k}:=\frac{\delta}{(k+r+1)^{1+\nu}}$ for any $\nu\geq 0$ and given $\delta\geq 0$ and $r>2$ .
In the sequel, we will denote by $\mathcal{S}_{K}:=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\delta_{k}$ and $\mathcal{S}_{\infty}:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\delta_{k}$ .
Adaptive mini-batch estimator.
One way to construct $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ is to use a mini-batch estimator with adaptively increasing size.
Given a mini-batch $\mathcal{B}_{k}$ of size $b_{k}$ , we construct
${\begin{array}[]{lcl}\widetilde{G}y^{k}&:=&\frac{1}{b_{k}}\sum_{\xi_{k}\in\mathcal{B}_{k}}\mathbf{G}(y^{k},\xi_{k}).\end{array}}\vskip-2.15277pt$
(16)
It is well-known that $\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}\big[\|\widetilde{G}y^{k}-Gy^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}}{b_{k}}$ , where $\sigma^{2}$ is the variance of the stochastic oracle $\mathbf{G}$ in Assumption 1.
To guarantee the condition (15), we need to choose $b_{k}$ adaptively.
For example, we can choose $b_{k}$ using the following condition:
${\begin{array}[]{lcl}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{b_{k}}\leq\frac{(1-\kappa)\sigma^{2}}{b_{k-1}}+\Theta L^{2}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}+\frac{\delta_{k}}{t_{k}^{2}}.\end{array}}\vskip-2.15277pt$
(17)
Clearly, by setting $\Delta_{k}:=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{b_{k}}$ , $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ defined by (16) satisfies (15).
Moreover, the condition (17) can be guaranteed by choosing an appropriate increasing mini-batch size $b_{k}$ .
This technique has been used in several previous works such as [21, 87].
Nevertheless, (15) can cover a wide range of estimators, including unbiased and biased instances, beyond the classical mini-batch instance (16).
3.2.2 Convergence rates in expectation.
Given $s>2$ , $\kappa$ and $\Theta$ in (15), we first define
$\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\Lambda_{0}&:=&\frac{s^{2}}{s^{2}-(1-\kappa)(s+1)^{2}}\left[\frac{25s-34}{2(s-2)}+\frac{9s^{2}+740s-1192}{64(s-1)}\right]\\
\omega&:=&\frac{9s^{2}+754s-1220}{256(s-1)}+\frac{2\Lambda_{0}\Theta(s+1)^{2}}{s^{2}},\\
\lambda&:=&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2(1+\omega)(s+1)}},\\
\mu&:=&\frac{s-2}{8(s-1)}\lambda.\end{array}\right.$
(18)
It is easy to check that these constants are positive and $\lambda\in(0,1)$ .
Now, let us state the convergence of (VAPEG) in Theorem 3.1 below for the recursive error bound condition (15).
The proof of Theorem 3.1 is given in Subsection 5.1.
Theorem 3.1
Let $\Lambda_{0}$ , $\mu$ , and $\lambda$ be defined by (18).
For (GE), suppose that $G$ satisfies Assumption 1 and Assumption 1 with $\alpha=1$ $($ i.e., $G$ is $L$ -Lipschitz continuous $)$ , and $\Phi$ satisfies Assumption 1 with $\rho_{n}\geq 0$ and $\rho_{c}=0$ $($ i.e., $\Phi$ is $\rho_{n}$ -co-hypomonotone $)$ such that $L\rho_{n}~<~\mu$ .
Let $\{(x^{k},y^{k},z^{k})\}$ be generated by (VAPEG) using an estimator $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ of $Gy^{k}$ satisfying the condition (15) with $\frac{2s+1}{(s+1)^{2}}<\kappa\leq 1$ for a given $s>2$
and the following parameters:
$\hskip-2.15277pt\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{8(s-1)\rho_{n}}{s-2}\leq\eta<\frac{\lambda}{L},\quad\gamma_{k}:=\frac{(s-2)\eta(k+s)}{32(s-1)(k+s+1)},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\beta_{k}:=\big[\frac{3(s-2)\eta}{16(s-1)}+2\rho_{n}]\frac{k+1}{k+s+1}-\frac{\gamma_{k}}{k+s+1}.\end{array}\hskip 0.0pt$
(19)
Then, the following statement holds:
$\mathrm{(a)}$
$($ Summability bounds $)$
There exist $v^{k}\in Tx^{k}$ for all $k\geq 0$ such that
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{(s-2)(4s-7)}{s-1}(k+1)\mathbb{E}\left[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\right]&\leq&64\big(\mathcal{R}_{0}^{2}+\Lambda_{0}\mathcal{S}_{K}\big),\\
\sum_{k=0}^{K}s(k+s+1)\mathbb{E}\left[\|Gy^{k}+v^{k+1}\|^{2}\right]&\leq&2\mathcal{R}_{0}^{2}+2\Lambda_{0}\mathcal{S}_{K},\end{array}$
(20)
where $\mathcal{S}_{K}:=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\delta_{k}$ and $\mathcal{R}_{0}^{2}$ is given by
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{R}_{0}^{2}&:=&\frac{2(13s-10)s^{2}-(s-1)(s-2)}{64(s-1)}\|Gx^{0}+v^{0}\|^{2}+\frac{16s^{3}(s-1)}{(s-2)\eta^{2}}\|x^{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}.\end{array}$
(21)
$\mathrm{(b)}$
$($ The $\mathcal{O}\left(1/k^{2}\right)$ convergence rates $)$ We have the following bounds:
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]&\leq&\dfrac{C_{0}^{2}(\mathcal{R}_{0}^{2}+\Lambda_{0}\mathcal{S}_{k})}{(k+s)^{2}}\\
\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gy^{k-1}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]&\leq&\dfrac{2C_{0}^{2}(\mathcal{R}_{0}^{2}+\Lambda_{0}\mathcal{S}_{k})}{(k+s)^{2}},\end{array}$
(22)
where $C_{0}^{2}:=\frac{128(s-1)}{19s+10}$ .
$\mathrm{(c)}$
$($ The $o\left(1/k^{2}\right)$ -convergence rates $)$
If additionally $\mathcal{S}_{\infty}:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\delta_{k}<+\infty$ , then
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\lim_{k\to\infty}k^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]=0\ \quad\textrm{and}\ \quad\lim_{k\to\infty}k^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gy^{k-1}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]=0.\end{array}$
(23)
$\mathrm{(d)}$
$($ Iteration-complexity $)$
For a given $\epsilon>0$ , if $\mathcal{S}_{\infty}<+\infty$ , then the number of iterations $k$ to achieve $\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}$ is at most $\mathcal{O}\big(1/\epsilon\big)$ .
This is also the number of evaluations of $J_{\eta T}$ .
3.2.3 Almost sure convergence.
Next, we state the almost sure convergence rates and the almost sure convergence of iterates in the following theorem, whose proof is given in Subsection 5.2.
Theorem 3.2
Under the same assumptions and settings as in Theorem 3.1, assuming that $\mathcal{S}_{\infty}:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\delta_{k}<+\infty$ , we have the following statements.
$\mathrm{(a)}$
The following summability results hold:
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+1)\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}&<&+\infty,\quad\textrm{almost surely},\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+s)\|Gy^{k-1}+v^{k}\|^{2}&<&+\infty,\quad\textrm{almost surely}.\end{array}$
(24)
$\mathrm{(b)}$
The following limits hold almost surely:
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\lim_{k\to\infty}k^{2}\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}=0\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{k\to\infty}k^{2}\|Gy^{k-1}+v^{k}\|^{2}=0.\end{array}$
(25)
$\mathrm{(c)}$
If, additionally, $\mathrm{gra}(\Phi)$ is closed, then $\{x^{k}\}$ almost surely converges to a $\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)$ -valued random variable $x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)$ .
In addition, the other two sequences of iterates $\{y^{k}\}$ and $\{z^{k}\}$ also almost surely converge to the same random variable $x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)$ .
Note that if Assumption 1 holds with $\rho_{c}=0$ , then Assumption 1 also automatically holds with $\rho_{*}=\rho_{n}$ .
Therefore, we do not require Assumption 1 in Theorems 3.1 and 3.2.
If $T$ is $\rho_{T}$ -co-hypomonotone for some $\rho_{T}\geq 0$ , then, as proven in [102], the resolvent $J_{\lambda T}$ is well-defined, single-valued, and nonexpansive if $\lambda>\rho_{T}$ .
Let us define the following forward-backward splitting (FBS) residual of (GE), widely used in monotone operator theory:
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda}x:=\frac{1}{\lambda}\big(x-J_{\lambda T}(x-\lambda Gx)\big).\end{array}$
(26)
It is well-known that $x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)$ iff $\mathcal{G}_{\lambda}x^{\star}=0$ .
Therefore, for a given tolerance $\epsilon>0$ , we can say that $x^{k}$ is an $\epsilon$ -solution of (GE) (in expectation) if $\mathbb{E}\big[\|\mathcal{G}_{\lambda}x^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}$ .
Since $\|\mathcal{G}_{\lambda}x^{k}\|\leq\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|$ for any $v^{k}\in Tx^{k}$ (see [105]), if we obtain $\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}$ as in Theorem 3.1, then we conclude that $x^{k}$ is also an $\epsilon$ -solution of (GE), i.e., $\mathbb{E}\big[\|\mathcal{G}_{\lambda}x^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}$ .
Remark 3.3
Note that if $\Phi$ is maximally monotone, then $\mathrm{gra}(\Phi)$ is closed, see [11, Proposition 20.38].
The closed-graph assumption is weaker than maximal monotonicity and is satisfied by many classes of continuous or closed set-valued mappings.
In the single-valued case, one can replace this closedness assumption of $\mathrm{gra}(\Phi)$ by a demiclosedness of $\Phi$ , see, e.g., [33].
If $T=0$ , then $\Phi=G$ , which is continuous, and thus $\mathrm{gra}(G)$ is closed.
3.3 Oracle complexity of VAPEG with adaptive mini-batching estimators.
Now, we estimate the oracle complexity of (VAPEG) using the adaptive mini-batch estimator $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ in (16) in the following corollary, whose proof can be found in Appendix C.5.
Corollary 3.4
Under the same conditions and settings as in Theorem 3.1, if $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ constructed by (16) with increasing mini-batch size $b_{k}:=\big\lceil\frac{\sigma^{2}(k+s+1)^{3+\nu}}{\delta}\big\rceil$ for some constants $\nu>0$ and $\delta>0$ is used in (VAPEG), then the expected total number of oracle calls $\mathbf{G}$ to achieve $x^{K}$ such that $\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{K}+v^{K}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}$ for a given tolerance $\epsilon>0$ and $v^{K}\in Tx^{K}$ is at most $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]=\mathcal{O}\big(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\epsilon^{4+\nu}}\big)$ .
The expected total number of $J_{\eta T}$ evaluations is at most $\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-1}\big)$ .
Alternatively, if we choose $b_{k}:=\big\lceil\frac{\sigma^{2}(k+s+1)^{3}\log(k+s+1)}{\delta}\big\rceil$ , then the above complexity is improved to $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\big(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\epsilon^{4}}\big)$ .
In this case, the expected total number of $J_{\eta T}$ evaluations is at most $\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-1})$ .
The work [21] studied VIPs, a special case of (GE), and established a $\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-4}\ln(\epsilon^{-1})\big)$ oracle complexity using increasing mini-batch estimators as (16).
Our complexity in Corollary 3.4 is either $\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-4+\nu}\big)$ for any sufficiently small $\nu>0$ or $\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-4}\ln(\ln(\epsilon^{-1}))\big)$ depending on the choice of $b_{k}$ , which is comparable with [21].
Nevertheless, our assumptions, Assumptions 1 and 1, are different from the pseudo-monotonicity in [21].
Moreover, as mentioned earlier, our method can work with any unbiased and biased estimators under the condition (15), beyond (16).
Increasing mini-batches were also used in [87], but the authors did not explicitly derive any oracle complexity, and also used different assumptions and metrics.
Note that our mini-batch size $b_{k}$ in Corollary 3.4 is loosely chosen from the condition (15) with $\kappa=1$ and $\Theta=0$ .
However, we can directly use the condition (17) to estimate $b_{k}$ , which gives us a smaller batch size than the worst case $b_{k}$ chosen in Corollary 3.4.
Corollary 3.4 also shows that if we use standard stochastic approximation (16) for $Gy^{k}$ , then our accelerated method (VAPEG) does not achieve better complexity than $\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-4})$ .
This is due to the accumulation of errors in accelerated methods compared to non-accelerated ones as studied in [35].
To achieve better complexity bounds, other variance-reduction techniques should be exploited.
For example, in Section 4 below, we will use control variate techniques.
4 Convergence and Complexity of VAPEG using Control Variate Estimators.
In this section, we will employ control variate techniques to develop a variance-reduction variant of (VAPEG).
Unlike Section 3, we let $\alpha\in[0,1)$ in Assumption 1 and require $\rho_{c}>0$ in Assumption 1.
4.1 A class of stochastic variance-reduced estimators.
We propose to use the following class of variance-reduced estimators $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ of $Gy^{k}$ , which was introduced in our recent work [102].
This class of estimators is relatively broad and covers various well-known instances, including Loopless SVRG, SAGA, and Loopless SARAH.
Definition 4.1
Given $\underline{\kappa}\in(0,1)$ and $\Theta>0$ , let $\{y^{k}\}$ be the sequence of iterates generated by (VAPEG) and $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ be a stochastic estimator of $Gy^{k}$ constructed from an i.i.d. sample $\mathcal{B}_{k}\subseteq[n]$ , adapted to the filtration $\{\mathcal{F}_{k}\}$ .
We say that $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ satisfies a $\textbf{VR}(\Delta_{k};\kappa_{k},\Theta_{k},\delta_{k})$ $($ variance-reduction $)$ property if there exist two parameters $\kappa_{k}\in[\underline{\kappa},1]$ and $\Theta_{k}\in[\underline{\Theta},\bar{\Theta}]$ , a nonnegative sequence $\left\{\delta_{k}\right\}$ , and a nonnegative random quantity $\Delta_{k}$ such that $($ almost surely $)$ :
${\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|\widetilde{G}y^{k}-Gy^{k}\|^{2}\big]&\leq&\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\Delta_{k}\big],\\
\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\Delta_{k}\big]&\leq&(1-\kappa_{k})\Delta_{k-1}+\Theta_{k}\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\|\mathbf{G}(y^{k},\xi)-\mathbf{G}(y^{k-1},\xi)\|^{2}\big]+\frac{\delta_{k}}{t_{k}^{2}},\end{array}\right.}\vskip-2.15277pt$
(27)
where $\{y^{k}\}$ and $\{t_{k}\}$ are updated by our scheme (VAPEG).
Definition 4.1 covers both unbiased and biased estimators $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ of $Gy^{k}$ since we do not impose the unbiased condition $\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}\big[\widetilde{G}y^{k}\big]=Gy^{k}$ .
It is also different from existing works, including [38, 100, 101], as we only require the condition (27), making it broader and allowing it to cover several existing estimators that may violate the definitions in [38, 100, 101].
Our class is also different from other generalizations such as [15, 44, 70] for stochastic optimization since they required the unbiasedness and additional or different conditions.
Now we provide three examples: Loopless SVRG, SAGA, and Loopless SARAH, that satisfy Definition 4.1.
Note that the Loopless SVRG and SAGA are unbiased, whereas the Loopless SARAH is biased.
In this paper, we only focus on these three estimators, but we believe that other variance-reduced estimators such as Hybrid SGD [106], SAG [64, 98], SARGE [39], SEGA [50], and JacSketch [46] could also be used in our methods.
4.1.1 Example 1: L-SVRG estimator.
SVRG was introduced in [54], and its Loopless version, called L-SVRG, was proposed in [62].
We show that this estimator satisfies Definition 4.1.
For given $G$ in (GE), two iterates $y^{k}$ and $\bar{y}^{k}$ , and i.i.d. sample $\mathcal{B}_{k}$ of size $b_{k}$ , we define $\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}(\cdot):=\frac{1}{b_{k}}\sum_{\xi_{k}\in\mathcal{B}_{k}}\mathbf{G}(\cdot,\xi_{k})$ is a mini-batch estimator, and then construct $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ as follows:
${\widetilde{G}y^{k}:=G\bar{y}^{k}+\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}y^{k}-\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}\bar{y}^{k},}\vskip-2.15277pt$
(L-SVRG)
where, for all $k\geq 1$ , $\bar{y}^{k}$ is updated by
${\bar{y}^{k}:=\begin{cases}y^{k-1}&\text{with probability}~\mathbf{p}_{k}\\
\bar{y}^{k-1}&\text{with probability}~1-\mathbf{p}_{k}.\end{cases}}\vskip-2.15277pt$
(28)
Here, $\mathbf{p}_{k}\in(0,1)$ is a given Bernoulli probability parameter, and $\bar{y}^{0}:=y^{0}$ .
The following lemma shows that $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ satisfies Definition 4.1, whose proof is given in [102].
Lemma 4.2
The estimator $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ constructed by (L-SVRG) such that $b_{k}\geq b_{k-1}$ satisfies Definition 4.1 with
$\kappa_{k}:=\frac{\mathbf{p}_{k}}{2}$ , $\Theta_{k}:=\frac{4}{b_{k}\mathbf{p}_{k}}$ , $\delta_{k}=0$ , and $\Delta_{k}:=\frac{1}{b_{k}}\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\|\mathbf{G}(y^{k},\xi)-\mathbf{G}(\bar{y}^{k},\xi)\|^{2}\big]$ .
4.1.2 Example 2: SAGA estimator.
SAGA was introduced in [34] for solving finite-sum convex optimization.
We apply it to (GE) when $G$ has a finite-sum structure as in (2).
For given $G$ in (2), an iterate sequence $\{y^{k}\}_{k\geq 0}$ , and a random mini-batch $\mathcal{B}_{k}\subseteq[n]:=\{1,\cdots,n\}$ of size $b_{k}$ sampled independently across iterations, for $k\geq 1$ , we update $\widehat{G}_{i}^{k}$ for all $i\in[n]$ as follows:
${\widehat{G}_{i}^{k}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}G_{i}y^{k-1}&\text{if}~i\in\mathcal{B}_{k},\\
\widehat{G}_{i}^{k-1}&\text{if}~i\notin\mathcal{B}_{k}\quad\textrm{(no update)}.\end{array}\right.}\vskip-2.15277pt$
(29)
Then, we construct a SAGA estimator $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ for $Gy^{k}$ as follows:
${\begin{array}[]{lcl}\widetilde{G}y^{k}&:=&\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{G}_{i}^{k}+G_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}y^{k}-\widehat{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}^{k},\end{array}}\vskip-2.15277pt$
(SAGA)
where $G_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}y^{k}:=\frac{1}{b_{k}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{k}}G_{i}y^{k}$ and $\widehat{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}^{k}:=\frac{1}{b_{k}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{k}}\widehat{G}_{i}^{k}$ .
In this case, we need to store $n$ component $\widehat{G}^{k}_{i}$ computed so far for all $i\in[n]$ in a table $\mathbb{T}_{k}:=[\widehat{G}_{1}^{k},\cdots,\widehat{G}_{n}^{k}]$ initialized at $\widehat{G}_{i}^{0}:=G_{i}y^{0}$ for all $i\in[n]$ .
SAGA requires significant memory to store $\mathbb{T}_{k}$ if $n$ and $p$ are both large.
We have the following result, whose proof can be found in [102].
Lemma 4.3
The estimator $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ constructed by (SAGA) such that $b_{k-1}-\frac{b_{k}b_{k-1}}{4n}\leq b_{k}\leq b_{k-1}$ satisfies Definition 4.1 with $\kappa_{k}:=\frac{b_{k}}{2n}$ , $\Theta_{k}:=\frac{5n}{b_{k}^{2}}$ , $\delta_{k}=0$ , and $\Delta_{k}:=\frac{1}{nb_{k}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}\|G_{i}y^{k}-\widehat{G}_{i}^{k}\|^{2}$ .
4.1.3 Example 3: L-SARAH estimator.
SARAH was introduced in [85] for finite-sum convex optimization.
Its loopless variant, called L-SARAH, was studied in [38, 67] for convex and nonconvex optimization, and recently in [24, 25, 102] for co-coercive root-finding problems.
This estimator is constructed as follows.
Given $G$ , $\{y^{k}\}$ , and an i.i.d. sample $\mathcal{B}_{k}\subseteq[n]$ of size $b_{k}$ , we construct $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ as
${\widetilde{G}y^{k}:=\begin{cases}\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}+\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}y^{k}-\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}y^{k-1}&\text{with probability}~1-\mathbf{p}_{k},\\
Gy^{k}&\text{with probability}~\mathbf{p}_{k},\end{cases}}\vskip-2.15277pt$
(L-SARAH)
where $\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}(\cdot):=\frac{1}{b_{k}}\sum_{\xi_{k}\in\mathcal{B}_{k}}\mathbf{G}(\cdot,\xi_{k})$ , $\widetilde{G}y^{0}:=Gy^{0}$ , and $\mathbf{p}_{k}\in[\underline{\mathbf{p}},1]$ is a given Bernoulli switching probability and $\underline{\mathbf{p}}\in(0,1)$ is a given lower bound.
The following lemma shows that $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ satisfies Definition 4.1, whose proof is presented in [102].
Lemma 4.4
The estimator $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ constructed by (L-SARAH) satisfies Definition 4.1 with
$\kappa_{k}:=\mathbf{p}_{k}$ , $\Theta_{k}:=\frac{1}{b_{k}}$ , $\delta_{k}=0$ , and $\Delta_{k}:=\|\widetilde{G}y^{k}-Gy^{k}\|^{2}$ .
4.2 Main result 2: Convergence properties of VAPEG using $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ in Definition 4.1.
For given $s>2$ , $\underline{\Theta}$ in Definition 4.1, and $L$ and $\alpha\in[0,1)$ in Assumption 1, we define
$\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\varphi_{s}&:=&\frac{9(85s-134)}{64(s-1)}+\frac{9(s-2)}{64},\\
\hat{\omega}&:=&\frac{s-2}{2(1-\alpha)(s-1)}+\frac{\varphi_{s}}{4}+\frac{s-2}{16(s-1)},\\
\hat{\lambda}&:=&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2(s+1)(1+\hat{\omega})}},\\
\hat{\mu}&:=&\frac{(s-2)\hat{\lambda}}{8(s-1)},\\
\Gamma&:=&\frac{3s^{2}}{s+1}\big[\varphi_{s}+\frac{25s-34}{2(s-2)}\big],\\
\hat{\Lambda}_{0}&:=&\frac{(s-2)\rho_{c}}{24(s^{2}-1)\rho_{n}\underline{\Theta}}.\end{array}\right.$
(30)
It is easy to check that these constants are positive and $\hat{\lambda}\in(0,1)$ .
Now, we are ready to state the convergence and convergence rates of (VAPEG), whose proof is deferred to Subsection 5.3 for the sake of presentation.
Theorem 4.5
Let $\hat{\mu}$ , $\hat{\lambda}$ , and $\Gamma$ be defined by (30).
Suppose that $G$ and $T$ in (GE) satisfy Assumptions 1, 1, 1, and 1 with $\alpha\in[0,1)$ , $\rho_{n}\geq\rho_{c}>0$ , $0\leq\rho_{*}\leq\rho_{n}$ , and $L\rho_{n}<\hat{\mu}$ .
Let $\{(x^{k},y^{k},z^{k})\}$ be generated by (VAPEG) using a variance-reduced estimator $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ of $Gy^{k}$ satisfying Definition 4.1 and $\eta$ , $\gamma_{k}$ , and $\beta_{k}$ such that:
$\hskip-1.07639pt\begin{array}[]{lcl}\frac{8(s-1)\rho_{n}}{s-2}\leq\eta<\frac{\hat{\lambda}}{L},\quad\gamma_{k}:=\frac{(s-2)\eta(k+s)}{32(s-1)(k+s+1)},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\beta_{k}:=\big[\frac{3(s-2)\eta}{16(s-1)}+2\rho_{n}]\frac{k+1}{k+s+1}-\frac{\gamma_{k}}{k+s+1}.\end{array}\hskip-1.07639pt$
(31)
Suppose further that both $\kappa_{k}$ and $\Theta_{k}$ in Definition 4.1 satisfy the following condition:
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\kappa_{k}\geq\frac{\eta\Gamma}{\rho_{c}}\Theta_{k}+\frac{2}{k+s+1}.\end{array}$
(32)
Then, the following statements hold:
$\mathrm{(a)}$
$($ The $\mathcal{O}\left(1/k^{2}\right)$ convergence rates $)$ We have the following bounds:
$\hskip-12.91663pt\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\dfrac{\hat{C}_{0}^{2}(\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}+\hat{\Lambda}_{0}\mathcal{S}_{k})}{(k+s)^{2}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gy^{k-1}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\dfrac{2\hat{C}_{0}^{2}(\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}+\hat{\Lambda}_{0}\mathcal{S}_{k})}{(k+s)^{2}},\end{array}\hskip-12.91663pt$
(33)
where $v^{k}\in Tx^{k}$ , $\mathcal{S}_{k}:=\sum_{l=0}^{k}\delta_{l}$ , and $\hat{C}_{0}^{2}$ and $\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}$ are respectively given by
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hat{C}_{0}^{2}&:=&\frac{128(s-1)}{19s+10}\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}:=\frac{2(13s-10)s^{2}-(s-1)(s-2)}{64(s-1)}\|Gx^{0}+v^{0}\|^{2}+\frac{16s^{3}(s-1)}{(s-2)\eta^{2}}\|x^{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}.\end{array}$
(34)
$\mathrm{(b)}$
$($ Summability bounds $)$
The following bounds also hold:
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\sum_{k=0}^{K}s(k+s+1)\mathbb{E}\left[\|Gy^{k}+v^{k+1}\|^{2}\right]&\leq&2(\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}+\hat{\Lambda}_{0}\mathcal{S}_{K}),\\
\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{(s-2)(4s-7)}{s-1}(k+1)\mathbb{E}\left[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\right]&\leq&64(\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}+\hat{\Lambda}_{0}\mathcal{S}_{K}).\end{array}$
(35)
$\mathrm{(c)}$
$($ The $o\left(1/k^{2}\right)$ -convergence rates $)$
If, in addition, $\mathcal{S}_{\infty}:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\delta_{k}<+\infty$ , then we also have the following limits:
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\lim_{k\to\infty}k^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]=0\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{k\to\infty}k^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gy^{k-1}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]=0.\end{array}$
(36)
$\mathrm{(d)}$
$($ Iteration-complexity $)$
For a given $\epsilon>0$ , if $\mathcal{S}_{\infty}<+\infty$ , then the number of iterations $k$ to achieve $\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}$ is at most $\mathcal{O}\big(1/\epsilon\big)$ .
This is also the number of evaluations of $J_{\eta T}$ .
$\mathrm{(e)}$
$($ Almost sure convergence $)$
The conclusions of Theorem 3.2 still hold in this case.
The constant $s$ in Theorem 4.5 was left free, provided that $s>2$ .
It provides flexibility in choosing its value for adjusting other parameters and theoretical bounds.
For example, if we assume that $\alpha=0$ in (3) and choose $s=3$ , then $\hat{\omega}=2.44336$ .
Consequently, we have $\hat{\lambda}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{27.54687}}\approx 0.19053$ and $\hat{\mu}:=\frac{(s-2)\hat{\lambda}}{8(s-1)}\approx 0.01191$ .
In this case, the conditions $L\rho_{n}\leq\hat{\mu}$ and $16\rho_{n}\leq\eta\leq\frac{\hat{\lambda}}{L}$ in Theorem 4.5 hold.
The supremum of the admissible stepsize is attained asymptotically as $s\downarrow 2$ , in which case $\eta\lesssim\frac{0.27123}{L}$ .
Note that these parameter bounds are not tight, and their range could potentially be improved by further refining our analysis.
Theorem 4.5 only establishes convergence rates for (VAPEG), but does not provide its oracle complexity.
It also requires the condition (32) to hold for $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ in Definition 4.1.
The iteration-complexity of (VAPEG) for achieving an $\epsilon$ -solution $x^{k}$ in expectation, i.e., $\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}$ is $\mathcal{O}\big(1/\epsilon\big)$ , improving by a factor of $\frac{1}{\epsilon}$ over its non-accelerated counterparts, including [101].
4.3 Oracle complexity of (VAPEG) using L-SVRG, SAGA, and L-SARAH.
In this subsection, we specify the oracle complexity of (VAPEG) using three concrete estimators: L-SVRG, SAGA, and L-SARAH in Subsection 4.1.
The key point is to choose an appropriate distribution $\mathbf{p}_{k}$ and a mini-batch size $b_{k}$ to fulfill the condition (32).
Note that from Lemmas 4.2, 4.3, and 4.4, since $\delta_{k}=0$ , we always have $\mathcal{S}_{\infty}:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\delta_{k}=0$ for these three estimators.
Now, let us start with the Loopless SVRG estimator in (L-SVRG).
Corollary 4.6
Suppose that Assumptions 1-1 hold for (GE) under the finite-sum setting (2).
Let $\{(x^{k},y^{k},z^{k})\}$ be generated by (VAPEG) using the SVRG estimator $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ from (L-SVRG) for $Gy^{k}$ and the parameters as in Theorem 4.5.
For given $\nu\in(0,1/2)$ and $c_{2}>0$ , if $n\geq\max\big\{\big[\frac{c_{2}(s+2)}{s-2}\big]^{1/\nu},\ \big[\frac{8\Gamma\eta}{c_{2}^{2}\rho_{c}}\big]^{1/(1-2\nu)}\big\}$ for $\Gamma$ in (30), and $b_{k}$ and $\mathbf{p}_{k}$ in (28) are chosen such that
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}b_{k}=b:=\big\lceil\frac{8\Gamma\eta n^{2\nu}}{c_{2}^{2}\rho_{c}}\big\rceil\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathbf{p}_{k}:=\begin{cases}\frac{c_{2}}{n^{\nu}}+\frac{4}{k+s+1}&\textrm{if}~k\leq\lfloor\frac{4n^{\nu}}{c_{2}}-s\rfloor,\\
\frac{2c_{2}}{n^{\nu}}&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}\end{array}$
(37)
Then, we have $1\leq b_{k}\leq n$ and $\mathbf{p}_{k}\in(0,1]$ .
For any $\epsilon>0$ , the expected total number of oracle calls $\mathbf{G}$ for $k\geq 0$ in (VAPEG) is at most
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]&=&\mathcal{O}\left(n\ln(n^{\nu})+n^{3\nu}+\frac{\mathcal{R}_{0}(n^{1-\nu}+n^{2\nu})}{\epsilon}\right)\end{array}$
(38)
to achieve $\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{K}+v^{K}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}$ .
If we choose $\nu=\frac{1}{3}$ , then $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]=\mathcal{O}\big(n\ln(n^{1/3})+\frac{n^{2/3}}{\epsilon}\big)$ .
Proof 4.7
Proof.
Since $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ is updated by (L-SVRG),
by Lemma 4.2, we have $\kappa_{k}=\frac{\mathbf{p}_{k}}{2}$ and $\Theta_{k}=\frac{4}{b_{k}\mathbf{p}_{k}}$ .
For $\Gamma$ given in (30), since $\kappa_{k}=\frac{\mathbf{p}_{k}}{2}$ and $\Theta_{k}=\frac{4}{b_{k}\mathbf{p}_{k}}$ , the condition (32) holds if
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbf{p}_{k}&\geq&\frac{8\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}b_{k}\mathbf{p}_{k}}+\frac{4}{k+s+1}.\end{array}$
Let $\nu\in[0,1]$ , $c_{1}>0$ , and $c_{2}>0$ be given.
If we choose $\mathbf{p}_{k}\geq\frac{c_{2}}{n^{\nu}}$ and $b_{k}\geq c_{1}n^{2\nu}$ , then the last condition holds if
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbf{p}_{k}&\geq&\frac{8\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{1}c_{2}n^{\nu}}}+\frac{4}{k+s+1}\geq\frac{8\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{1}c_{2}n^{\nu}}}=:\underline{\mathbf{p}}>0.\end{array}$
(39)
Therefore, if we choose $c_{1}=\frac{8\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{2}^{2}}}$ , then $\frac{8\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{1}c_{2}n^{\nu}}}=\frac{c_{2}}{n^{\nu}}$ .
Moreover, if $k\geq\frac{4n^{\nu}}{c_{2}}-s$ , then $\frac{4}{k+s+1}\leq\frac{c_{2}}{n^{\nu}}$ .
Overall, if we choose $\mathbf{p}_{k}$ as in (37), then it satisfies (39).
On the other hand, to guarantee $\mathbf{p}_{k}\leq 1$ , we require $\frac{2c_{2}}{n^{\nu}}\leq 1$ and $\frac{c_{2}}{n^{\nu}}+\frac{4}{s+2}\leq 1$ , which are guaranteed if $n\geq\big[\frac{c_{2}(s+2)}{s-2}\big]^{1/\nu}$ .
In addition, we need $\frac{8\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{2}^{2}}}n^{2\nu}=c_{1}n^{2\nu}\leq b\leq n$ , which holds if $n\geq\big[\frac{8\Gamma}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{2}^{2}}}\big]^{1/(1-2\nu)}$ .
Combining these two conditions, we get $n\geq\max\big\{\big[\frac{c_{2}(s+2)}{s-2}\big]^{1/\nu},\ \big[\frac{8\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{2}^{2}}}\big]^{1/(1-2\nu)}\big\}$ .
In this case, we can choose $b:=\big\lceil\frac{8\Gamma\eta n^{2\nu}}{\rho_{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{2}^{2}}}\big\rceil$ as in (37).
We can also easily verify that $b\geq 1$ .
Now, from (33) of Theorem 4.5 and $S_{K}=0$ , to guarantee $\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{K}+v^{K}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}$ , we impose $\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{C}_{0}^{2}\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}}}{(K+s)^{2}}\leq\epsilon^{2}$ for $\hat{C}_{0}^{2}:=\frac{128(s-1)}{(19s+10)}$ .
This leads to the choice $K:=\big\lfloor\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{C}_{0}\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}}}{\epsilon}\big\rfloor$ .
The expected total number of evaluations of $\mathbf{G}(y^{k},\xi_{k})$ is $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]=n+\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{K}\big]$ , where $n$ is the total evaluations of the first epoch ( $k=0$ ), and $\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{K}$ is the evaluations of the next $K$ iterations.
Using (37), we can evaluate the expected total number $\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{K}\big]$ of oracle calls $\mathbf{G}$ as follows:
${\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{K}\big]&:=&\sum_{k=1}^{K}[n\mathbf{p}_{k}+2(1-\mathbf{p}_{k})b]\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}(n\mathbf{p}_{k}+2b)\\
&\leq&\sum_{k=1}^{\frac{4n^{\nu}}{c_{2}}-s}\big(c_{2}n^{1-\nu}+\frac{4n}{k+s+1}+2c_{1}n^{2\nu}\big)+\big(K-\frac{4n^{\nu}}{c_{2}}+s)(2c_{2}n^{1-\nu}+2c_{1}n^{2\nu})\\
&\leq&4n+4n\ln\big(\frac{4n^{\nu}}{c_{2}}+2\big)+c_{1}n^{3\nu}+\frac{(2c_{2}n^{1-\nu}+2c_{1}n^{2\nu}){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{C}_{0}\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}}}{\epsilon}\\
&=&\mathcal{O}\left(n\ln(n^{\nu})+n^{3\nu}+\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}}(n^{1-\nu}+n^{2\nu})}{\epsilon}\right).\end{array}}\vskip-2.15277pt$
This expression, together with $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]=n+\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{K}\big]$ , leads to (38).
Clearly, if $\nu=\frac{1}{3}$ , then we get $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]=\mathcal{O}\big(n\ln(n^{1/3})+\frac{n^{2/3}}{\epsilon}\big)$ .
$\square$
Our next result is the complexity of (VAPEG) using the SAGA estimator (SAGA).
Corollary 4.8
Suppose that Assumptions 1-1 hold for (GE) under the finite-sum setting (2).
Let $\{(x^{k},y^{k},z^{k})\}$ be generated by (VAPEG) using the SAGA estimator $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ from (SAGA) for $Gy^{k}$ and the parameters as in Theorem 4.5.
Suppose that $n\geq\frac{10\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}}\big(\frac{s+2}{s-2}\big)^{3}$ and we choose $b_{k}$ in (SAGA) as
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}b_{k}:=\begin{cases}\big\lceil\sqrt[3]{\frac{10\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}}}n^{2/3}+\frac{4n}{k+s+1}\big\rceil&\textrm{if}~k\leq\big\lfloor\frac{4\sqrt[3]{\rho_{c}}}{\sqrt[3]{10\Gamma\eta}}n^{1/3}-s\big\rfloor,\\
\big\lceil 2\sqrt[3]{\frac{10\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}}}n^{2/3}\big\rceil&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}\end{array}$
(40)
Then, we have $1\leq b_{k}\leq n$ , and for any $\epsilon>0$ , the expected total number of oracle calls $\mathbf{G}$ in (VAPEG) is at most
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]&=&\mathcal{O}\left(n\ln(n^{1/3})+\frac{\mathcal{R}_{0}n^{2/3}}{\epsilon}\right)\end{array}$
(41)
to achieve $\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{K}+v^{K}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}$ .
Proof 4.9
Proof.
