As indicated by Lemma 4.3, $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ generated by (SAGA) satisfies Definition 4.1 with $\kappa_{k}:=\frac{b_{k}}{2n}$ and $\Theta_{k}:=\frac{5n}{b_{k}^{2}}$ .
For given $\nu\in[0,1/2]$ and $c_{1}>0$ , let us choose $b_{k}$ as
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}b_{k}&=&\begin{cases}c_{1}n^{2\nu}+\frac{4n}{k+s+1}&\textrm{if $k\leq\lfloor\frac{4n^{1-2\nu}}{c_{1}}-s-1\rfloor$},\\
2c_{1}n^{2\nu}&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}\end{array}$
(42)
Since $\kappa_{k}:=\frac{b_{k}}{2n}$ and $\Theta_{k}:=\frac{5n}{b_{k}^{2}}$ , the condition (32) holds if $b_{k}\geq\frac{10n^{2}\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}b_{k}^{2}}+\frac{4n}{k+s+1}$ .
Because $b_{k}$ is updated by (42), the last condition is satisfied if $b_{k}\geq\frac{10\Gamma\eta n^{2(1-2\nu)}}{\rho_{c}c_{1}^{2}}+\frac{4n}{k+s+1}$ , which automatically holds if we impose $\frac{10n^{2(1-2\nu)}\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}c_{1}^{2}}=c_{1}n^{2\nu}$ .
The last equality holds if $\nu=\frac{1}{3}$ and $c_{1}:=\sqrt[3]{\frac{10\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}}}$ .
Substituting these values into (42), we obtain $b_{k}$ as in (40).
From (32), we can observe that $\Gamma\eta>\rho_{c}$ , leading to $b\geq 1$ .
Since $b_{k}\leq n$ , we need to impose $\sqrt[3]{\frac{10\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}}}n^{2/3}\leq\frac{n(s-2)}{s+2}$ , leading to $n\geq\frac{10\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}}\big(\frac{s+2}{s-2}\big)^{3}$ .
In addition, for $b_{k}$ in (40), we can easily check that it satisfies $b_{k-1}-\frac{b_{k}b_{k-1}}{4n}\leq b_{k}\leq b_{k-1}$ in Lemma 4.3.
Now, for a given tolerance $\epsilon>0$ , from (33) of Theorem 4.5, to guarantee that $\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{K}+v^{K}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}$ , we need to impose $\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{C}_{0}^{2}\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}}}{(K+s)^{2}}\leq\epsilon^{2}$ for $\hat{C}_{0}^{2}:=\frac{128(s-1)}{(19s+10)}$ .
This requirement leads to the choice $K:=\big\lfloor\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{C}_{0}\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}}}{\epsilon}\big\rfloor$ .
Let us estimate the expected total number $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]$ of oracle calls $\mathbf{G}$ for (VAPEG), which is $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]=n+\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{K}\big]$ , where
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{K}\big]&=&2\sum_{k=1}^{K}b_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{\frac{4n^{1/3}}{c_{1}}-s}\big(c_{1}n^{2/3}+\frac{4n}{k+s+1}\big)+4c_{1}n^{2/3}\big(K-\frac{4n^{1/3}}{c_{1}}+s\big)\\
&\leq&4n+4n\ln\big(\frac{4n^{1/3}}{c_{1}}+1\big)+\frac{4c_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{C}_{0}\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}}n^{2/3}}{\epsilon}\\
&=&\mathcal{O}\left(n\ln(n^{1/3})+\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}}n^{2/3}}{\epsilon}\right).\end{array}$
This implies that $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]=n+\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{K}\big]\leq\mathcal{O}\big(n\ln(n^{1/3})+\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}}n^{2/3}}{\epsilon}\big)$ as stated in (41).
$\square$
Finally, we estimate the complexity of (VAPEG) using the L-SARAH estimator.
Corollary 4.10
Suppose that Assumptions 1-1 hold for (GE) under the finite-sum setting (2).
Let $\{(x^{k},y^{k},z^{k})\}$ be generated by (VAPEG) using the SARAH estimator $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ from (L-SARAH) for $Gy^{k}$ and the parameters as in Theorem 4.5.
For given $\nu\in(0,1]$ and $c_{1}\in(0,n^{1-\nu}]$ , if $n\geq\big[\frac{2\Gamma\eta}{c_{1}\rho_{c}}\big]^{1/\nu}$ for $\Gamma$ given in (30), and $b$ and $\mathbf{p}_{k}$ in (L-SARAH) are chosen as
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}b_{k}=b:=\lceil c_{1}n^{\nu}\rceil\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathbf{p}_{k}:=\begin{cases}\frac{\Gamma\eta}{c_{1}\rho_{c}n^{\nu}}+\frac{2}{k+s+1}&\textrm{if}~k\leq\lfloor\frac{2c_{1}\rho_{c}n^{\nu}}{\Gamma\eta}-s\rfloor,\\
\frac{2\Gamma\eta}{c_{1}\rho_{c}n^{\nu}}&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}\end{array}$
(43)
Then, we have $1\leq b_{k}\leq n$ and $\mathbf{p}_{k}\in(0,1]$ .
For any given $\epsilon>0$ , the expected total number of oracle calls $\mathbf{G}$ is at most
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]&=&\mathcal{O}\left(n\ln(n^{\nu})+n^{2\nu}+\frac{\mathcal{R}_{0}(n^{1-\nu}+n^{\nu})}{\epsilon}\right)\end{array}$
(44)
to achieve $\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{K}+v^{K}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}$ .
If we choose $\nu=\frac{1}{2}$ , then $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]=\mathcal{O}\big(n\ln(n^{1/2})+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon}\big)$ .
Proof 4.11
Proof.
By Lemma 4.4, $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ generated by (L-SARAH) satisfies Definition 4.1 with $\kappa_{k}=\mathbf{p}_{k}$ and $\Theta_{k}=\frac{1}{b_{k}}$ .
Using these values, the condition (32) holds if
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbf{p}_{k}\geq\frac{\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}b_{k}}+\frac{2}{k+s+1}.\end{array}$
Let us choose $b:=\lceil c_{1}n^{\nu}\rceil$ as in (43) for some $\nu\in(0,1)$ and $c_{1}>0$ .
Then, the last condition holds if we choose $\mathbf{p}_{k}$ as in (43).
Moreover, to ensure $0<\mathbf{p}_{k}\leq 1$ , we need to impose $b_{k}\geq c_{1}n^{\nu}\geq\frac{2\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}}\geq 1$ , leading to $n\geq\big[\frac{2\Gamma\eta}{c_{1}\rho_{c}}\big]^{1/\nu}$ as stated.
In addition, we also have $b_{k}\leq n$ .
Now, from (33) of Theorem 4.5, to guarantee that $\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{K}+v^{K}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}$ , we need to impose $\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{C}_{0}^{2}\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}}}{(K+s)^{2}}\leq\epsilon^{2}$ for $\hat{C}_{0}^{2}:=\frac{128(s-1)}{(19s+10)}$ .
This requirement leads to the choice of $K:=\big\lfloor\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{C}_{0}\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}}}{\epsilon}\big\rfloor$ .
In this case, the expected total number of oracle calls $\mathbf{G}$ is $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]=n+\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{K}\big]$ , where $n$ is the number of evaluations in the first epoch $k=0$ , and $\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{K}$ is the number of evaluations over the next $K$ iterations.
Then, we can evaluate $\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{K}\big]$ as
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{K}\big]&:=&\sum_{k=1}^{K}[n\mathbf{p}_{k}+2(1-\mathbf{p}_{k})b]\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}(n\mathbf{p}_{k}+2b)\\
&\leq&\sum_{k=1}^{\frac{2c_{1}n^{\nu}}{\Gamma}-s}\big(\frac{\Gamma n^{1-\nu}}{c_{1}}+\frac{2n}{k+s+1}+2c_{1}n^{\nu}\big)+\big(K-\frac{2c_{1}n^{\nu}}{\Gamma}\big)\big(\frac{2\Gamma n^{1-\nu}}{c_{1}}+2c_{1}n^{\nu}\big)\\
&\leq&2n+2n\ln\big(\frac{2c_{1}n^{\nu}}{\Gamma}+2\big)+\frac{4c_{1}^{2}n^{2\nu}}{\Gamma}+\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{C}_{0}\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}}}{\epsilon}\big(\frac{2n^{1-\nu}}{\Gamma}+2c_{1}n^{\nu}\big)\\
&=&\mathcal{O}\left(n\ln(n^{\nu})+n^{2\nu}+\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}}(n^{1-\nu}+n^{\nu})}{\epsilon}\right).\end{array}$
This expression together with $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]=n+\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{K}\big]$ leads to (44).
Finally, if we choose $\nu=\frac{1}{2}$ , then $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]=\mathcal{O}\big(n\ln(n^{1/2})+\frac{n^{1/2}}{\epsilon}\big)$ .
$\square$
Remark 4.12
Note that both $\mathbf{p}_{k}$ and $b_{k}$ selected in Corollaries 4.6, 4.8, and 4.10 aim at achieving the best oracle complexity.
Other choices of these parameters still work as long as they satisfy the condition $\kappa_{k}\geq\frac{\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}}\Theta_{k}+\frac{2}{k+s+1}$ in Theorem 4.5.
For instance, the probability $\mathbf{p}_{k}$ in Corollary 4.10 can be fixed at $\mathbf{p}_{k}:=\frac{\Gamma\eta}{c_{1}\rho_{c}\sqrt{n}}+\frac{2}{s+1}$ for all $k\geq 1$ and $s>2$ .
This choice is more convenient for the implementation of (VAPEG) in practice.
Remark 4.13
Compared with [101], Assumption 1 is more restrictive than the weak-Minty condition in [101].
However, (VAPEG) achieves a better convergence rate by a factor of $\frac{1}{k}$ and better oracle complexity by a factor $\frac{1}{\epsilon}$ .
Moreover, (VAPEG) also works with biased estimators.
For SARAH, it improves by a factor $n^{1/6}$ over SVRG and SAGA.
Compared to [1, 2, 25], (VAPEG) can work with a class of nonmonotone operators, and its convergence guarantee is in $\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{K}+v^{K}\|^{2}\big]$ ,
whereas the gap function guarantees in [1, 2] apply only to the monotone case.
Therefore, a direct comparison of their oracle complexities is not meaningful because the convergence metrics differ.
Nevertheless, since the methods in [1, 2] are non-accelerated, we believe that their convergence rate is at most $\mathcal{O}\big(1/k\big)$ , leading to an oracle complexity worse by a factor of $\frac{1}{\epsilon}$ than ours.
Other works such as [33, 100, 102] require stronger assumptions such as [star] co-coercivity and strong quasi-monotonicity, excluding our problems of interest.
Remark 4.14
We only consider the finite-sum case (2) in Corollaries 4.6, 4.8, and 4.10.
If we consider the general case $G(\cdot)=\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\mathbf{G}(\cdot,\xi)\big]$ , then we can also prove that the expected total number of oracle calls $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]$ is at most $\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-3})$ to achieve an $\epsilon$ -solution.
However, we skip this analysis.
5 The Detailed Proofs of Theorems 3.1, 3.2, and 4.5.
For readability, we present the technical proofs of Theorems 3.1, 3.2, and 4.5 in this section.
5.1 The full proof of Theorem 3.1.
The proof of Theorem 3.1 is divided into several steps.
5.1.1 Key technical lemmas for convergence analysis.
For given $x^{k}$ , $y^{k-1}$ , $y^{k}$ , and $v^{k}\in Tx^{k}$ , we introduce the following quantities of our analysis:
$\begin{array}[]{lclclcl}\hat{w}^{k}&:=&Gy^{k-1}+v^{k}&\textrm{and}&\quad w^{k}&:=&Gx^{k}+v^{k},\\
e^{k}&:=&\widetilde{G}y^{k}-Gy^{k}&\textrm{and}&\quad\hat{e}^{k}&:=&\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}-Gx^{k}.\end{array}$
(45)
Our first step is to establish the following key lemma, whose proof is in Appendix B.1.
Lemma 5.1
Assume that Assumptions 1 and 1 hold for (GE).
Let $\{(x^{k},y^{k},z^{k})\}$ be updated by (VAPEG) using the following parameters:
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}t_{k+1}:=t_{k}+1,\quad\gamma_{k}:=\frac{\gamma(t_{k}-1)}{t_{k}},\quad\text{and}\quad\beta_{k}:=\frac{(c_{1}\eta+c_{2}\eta+2\rho_{n})(t_{k}-s)-\gamma_{k}}{t_{k}},\end{array}$
(46)
for some constants $\gamma~>~0$ , $c_{1}>0$ , and $c_{2}>0$ , determined later, and $t_{0}\geq s+1$ for a given $s>2$ .
Let $w^{k}$ , $\hat{w}^{k}$ , $e^{k}$ , and $\hat{e}^{k}$ be defined by (45), $v^{k}\in Tx^{k}$ , and $\mathcal{P}_{k}$ be defined by
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{P}_{k}:=\frac{a_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+st_{k-1}\langle w^{k},x^{k}-z^{k}\rangle+\frac{s^{2}(s-1)}{2\gamma_{k-1}}\|z^{k}-x^{\star}\|^{2}.\end{array}$
(47)
Then, for any constant $\omega\geq 0$ and $\theta>0$ , we have
$\hskip-17.22217pt\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{P}_{k}-\mathcal{P}_{k+1}&\geq&\frac{\eta t_{k}^{2}}{2}\big[(1-\alpha)\big(|\!\|w^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k+1}|\!\|^{2}-\|w^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}\big)+\omega L^{2}\|x^{k+1}-y^{k}\|^{2}\big]\\
&&+{~}\rho_{c}t_{k}(t_{k}-s)|\!\|Gx^{k+1}-Gx^{k}|\!\|^{2}+\frac{\eta t_{k}(t_{k}-s-M^{2}\eta^{2}t_{k})}{2}\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-w^{k}\|^{2}\\
&&+{~}\frac{\eta st_{k}}{2}\|\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}+\frac{a_{k}-\hat{a}_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+s(s-1)\langle w^{k},x^{k}-x^{\star}\rangle\\
&&-{~}b_{k}\|e^{k}\|^{2}-\hat{b}_{k}\|\hat{e}^{k}\|^{2},\end{array}\hskip-17.22217pt$
(48)
where $M^{2}:=2(1+\omega)L^{2}$ , $\psi:=(1-c_{1}-c_{2})\eta-2\rho_{n}$ , and
$\hskip 0.0pt\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}a_{k+1}&:=&\psi t_{k}^{2}+2s\rho_{n}t_{k}-\gamma(t_{k}-1),\\
\hat{a}_{k}&:=&\psi(t_{k}-s)^{2}+s(\eta+2\rho_{n})(t_{k}-s)+\frac{2\gamma{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(t_{k}-1)^{2}}}{t_{k}},\\
b_{k}&:=&\big(\frac{1}{2c_{1}}+\frac{(1+\theta)M^{2}\eta^{2}}{2}\big)\eta t_{k}^{2},\\
\hat{b}_{k}&:=&\big(\frac{\beta_{k}^{2}t_{k}}{2c_{2}\eta}+\frac{\gamma(t_{k}-1)}{2t_{k}}+\frac{(1+\theta^{-1})M^{2}\eta^{3}t_{k}}{2}\big)t_{k}+\frac{\gamma(s-1)(t_{k}-1)}{2}\big(\frac{2}{t_{k}}+t_{k}-2\big).\end{array}\right.\hskip 0.0pt$
(49)
Next, we can further simplify Lemma 5.1 by choosing concrete values for the constants $c_{1}$ and $c_{2}$ to achieve a simplified bound for $\mathcal{P}_{k}$ as stated in the following lemma.
Lemma 5.2
Under the same conditions and parameters as in Lemma 5.1, suppose that
$\hskip-4.30554pt\begin{array}[]{lcl}s\ >\ 2,\qquad t_{k}\geq s+1,\qquad\frac{M^{2}\eta^{2}t_{k}}{t_{k}-s}\leq 1,\qquad 0<\gamma\leq\frac{9}{16}\eta,\quad\textrm{and}\quad(s-2)\eta\geq 8(s-1)\rho_{n}.\end{array}\hskip-12.91663pt$
(50)
Then, $\beta_{k}$ in (46) satisfies $\beta_{k}\leq\big[\frac{3(s-2)\eta}{16(s-1)}+2\rho_{n}]\frac{(t_{k}-s)}{t_{k}}$ and $\mathcal{P}_{k}$ defined by (47) satisfies
$\hskip-2.15277pt\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{P}_{k}-\mathcal{P}_{k+1}&\geq&\frac{\eta t_{k}^{2}}{2}\big[(1-\alpha)\big(|\!\|w^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k+1}|\!\|^{2}-\|w^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}\big)+\omega L^{2}\|x^{k+1}-y^{k}\|^{2}\big]\\
&&+{~}\rho_{c}t_{k}(t_{k}-s)|\!\|Gx^{k+1}-Gx^{k}|\!\|^{2}+\frac{\eta t_{k}(t_{k}-s)}{2}\big(1-\frac{M^{2}\eta^{2}t_{k}}{t_{k}-s}\big)\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-w^{k}\|^{2}\\
&&+{~}\frac{\eta st_{k}}{2}\|\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}+\frac{\phi_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+s(s-1)\langle w^{k},x^{k}-x^{\star}\rangle\\
&&-{~}\frac{\varphi\eta t_{k-1}^{2}}{8}\|w^{k}-\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}-\frac{(25s-34)\eta t_{k}^{2}}{2(s-2)}\|e^{k}\|^{2}-\varphi\eta t_{k-1}^{2}\|e^{k-1}\|^{2},\end{array}\hskip-8.61108pt$
(51)
where
$\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\phi_{k}&:=&\big[\frac{5(s-2)}{8}\eta-4(s-1)\rho_{n}-3\gamma\big](t_{k}-1)+\frac{(s-1)(3s+10)\eta}{16}+2(2s^{2}-3s+1)\rho_{n}+\gamma,\\
\varphi&:=&\frac{9(85s-134)}{64(s-1)}+\frac{9(s-1)\gamma}{2\eta}.\end{array}\right.$
(52)
Remark 5.3
Lemma 5.2 only provides one choice for the constants $c_{1}$ and $c_{2}$ .
This choice affects the ranges of parameters $\eta$ , $\beta_{k}$ , and $\gamma_{k}$ in (VAPEG) as well as the range of $L\rho_{n}$ .
In this paper, we do not try to optimize the choice of these constants.
We only provide one instance to simplify our convergence analysis.
However, other choices are also possible.
Finally, for $\mathcal{P}_{k}$ defined by (47), we can lower bound it as in the following lemma.
Lemma 5.4
Under the same settings as in Lemma 5.2 and Assumption 1, $\mathcal{P}_{k}$ defined by (47) is lower bounded by
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{P}_{k}&\geq&\frac{A_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{s^{2}}{2}\big(\frac{s-1}{\gamma}-\frac{4}{\eta}\big)\|z^{k}-x^{\star}\|^{2},\end{array}$
(53)
where $A_{k}:=\big[\frac{3(3s-2)}{16(s-1)}\eta-2\rho_{n}\big]t_{k-1}^{2}+2s(\rho_{n}-\rho_{*})t_{k-1}-\gamma(t_{k-1}-1)$ .
5.1.2 The Lyapunov function and additional supporting lemmas.
We consider the following Lyapunov function:
$\hskip-2.15277pt\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{L}_{k}&:=&\mathcal{P}_{k}+\frac{\omega\eta L^{2}t_{k-1}^{2}}{2}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}\eta(1-\kappa)(s+1)^{2}t_{k-1}^{2}}{s^{2}}\Delta_{k-1}+\big(\varphi+\frac{s-2}{32(s-1)}\big)\eta t_{k-1}^{2}\|e^{k-1}\|^{2},\end{array}\hskip-4.30554pt$
(54)
where $\Lambda_{0}$ is given in (18), $\kappa$ is in (15), $\varphi$ is in (52), and $\mathcal{P}_{k}$ is the function defined by (47).
Next, we need the following supporting lemmas to simplify the proof of Theorem 3.1.
The proof of these lemmas can be found in Appendix C.
Lemma 5.5
Under the same settings as in Theorem 3.1, $\mathcal{L}_{k}$ given in (54) satisfies
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{L}_{k}-\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\mathcal{L}_{k+1}\big]&\geq&\frac{\epsilon_{1}\eta t_{k}(t_{k}-s)}{2}\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-w^{k}\|^{2}\big]+\frac{\eta st_{k}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}\big]\\
&&+{~}\frac{(s-2)(4s-7)\eta(k+1)}{64(s-1)}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{(s-2)\eta L^{2}t_{k-1}^{2}}{32(s-1)}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}\\
&&+{~}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(2s+1)\Lambda_{0}\eta(1-\kappa)t_{k-1}(t_{k-1}-s)}}{s^{2}}\Delta_{k-1}+\frac{(s-2)\eta t_{k-1}^{2}}{32(s-1)}\|e^{k-1}\|^{2}-\Lambda_{0}\eta\delta_{k},\end{array}$
(55)
where $\epsilon_{1}:=1-(s+1)M^{2}\eta^{2}\geq 0$ and $\Lambda_{0}$ is given in (18).
Lemma 5.6
Let $\{(x^{k},y^{k},z^{k})\}$ be generated by (VAPEG) and the conditions of Lemmas 5.2 and 5.4 hold.
Suppose further that
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}\right]&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[|\!\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k}|\!\|^{2}\big]&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|\hat{e}^{k}\|^{2}\big]&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|e^{k}\|^{2}\big]&<&+\infty.\end{array}$
(56)
Then, we have
$\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\big[\|x^{k}-z^{k}\|^{2}\big]=0,\ \ \textrm{and}\ \ \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\tfrac{1}{t_{k}}\mathbb{E}\big[\|z^{k}-x^{k}\|^{2}\big]<+\infty.$
(57)
In addition, we also have
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|x^{k+1}-y^{k}\|^{2}\big]&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}\mathbb{E}\big[\|y^{k}-x^{k}\|^{2}\big]&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}\mathbb{E}\big[\|x^{k+1}-x^{k}\|^{2}\big]&<&+\infty.\end{array}$
(58)
Lemma 5.7
Under the same settings as in Lemma 5.6, for $\mathcal{P}_{k}$ given in (47), if $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{P}_{k}\big]\leq\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{0}$ for all $k\geq 0$ and a given $\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{0}\geq 0$ , then $\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\big[\|x^{k}-x^{\star}\|^{2}\big]$ exists for any $x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)$ .
If, additionally, $\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{P}_{k}\big]$ exists, $\{t_{k-1}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|w^{k}\|^{2}\big]\}$ is bounded, and $\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k-1}\mathbb{E}\big[\|w^{k}\|^{2}\big]<+\infty$ , then
$\lim_{k\to\infty}t_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|w^{k}\|^{2}\big]=0\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{k\to\infty}t_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}\big]=0.$
(59)
5.1.3 The proof of Theorem 3.1.
For clarity, we divide the proof into the following steps.
Step 1 (The summability results in (20)).
First, applying Lemma 5.5, it follows from (55) after taking the full expectation that
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{L}_{k}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{L}_{k+1}\big]&\geq&\frac{\eta s(k+s+1)}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}\right]+\frac{(s-2)(4s-7)\eta(k+1)}{64(s-1)}\mathbb{E}\big[\|w^{k}\|^{2}\big]\\
&&+{~}\frac{(2s+1)\Lambda_{0}\eta{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(1-\kappa)}t_{k-1}(t_{k-1}-s)}{s^{2}}\mathbb{E}\big[\Delta_{k-1}\big]\\
&&+{~}\frac{\epsilon_{1}\eta t_{k}(t_{k}-s)}{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-w^{k}\|^{2}\big]+\frac{(s-2)\eta L^{2}t_{k-1}^{2}}{32(s-1)}\mathbb{E}\big[\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}\big]\\
&&+{~}\frac{(s-2)\eta t_{k-1}^{2}}{32(s-1)}\mathbb{E}\big[\|e^{k-1}\|^{2}\big]-\Lambda_{0}\eta\delta_{k}.\end{array}$
(60)
Moreover, from Lemma 5.4, (54), and the nonnegativity of the coefficients, we have $\mathcal{L}_{k}\geq 0$ for all $k\geq 0$ .
Summing up (60) from $k=0$ to $k:=K$ and using $\mathcal{L}_{k}\geq 0$ and $\mathcal{S}_{K}:=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\delta_{k}$ , we obtain
$\begin{array}[]{lclcl}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\eta s(k+s+1)\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}\right]&\leq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2\mathcal{L}_{0}+2\Lambda_{0}\eta\mathcal{S}_{K}},\\
\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{(s-2)(4s-7)\eta}{s-1}(k+1)\mathbb{E}\left[\|w^{k}\|^{2}\right]&\leq&64\mathcal{L}_{0}+64\Lambda_{0}\eta\mathcal{S}_{K},\\
\end{array}$
(61)
Since $z^{0}=x^{0}=y^{-1}$ and the conventions $e^{-1}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta_{-1}}=0$ , we have from (54) that
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{L}_{0}&=&\mathcal{P}_{0}=\frac{a_{0}}{2}\|w^{0}\|^{2}+\frac{s^{2}(s-1)}{2\gamma_{-1}}\|x^{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}\\
&=&\frac{[2(13s-10)s^{2}-(s-1)(s-2)]\eta}{64(s-1)}\|w^{0}\|^{2}+\frac{16s^{3}(s-1)}{(s-2)\eta}\|x^{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}.\end{array}$
Here, we have used $a_{0}=\left[\frac{13s-10}{16(s-1)}\eta-2\rho_{n}\right]s^{2}+2s^{2}\rho_{n}-\gamma(s-1)=\frac{[2(13s-10)s^{2}-(s-1)(s-2)]\eta}{32(s-1)}>0$ for all $s>2$ and $\gamma_{-1}=\frac{(s-2)\eta(t_{-1}-1)}{32(s-1)t_{-1}}=\frac{{(s-2)\eta}}{32s}$ .
Substituting this $\mathcal{L}_{0}$ into (61), we obtain (20).
Step 2 (The proof of the bounds in (22)).
Now, from (60), by induction, we also have $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{L}_{k}\big]\leq\mathcal{L}_{0}+\Lambda_{0}\eta\mathcal{S}_{k}$ .
However, from (53) and (54), we also have
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{L}_{k}\geq\mathcal{P}_{k}&\geq&\frac{A_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{s^{2}}{2}\big(\frac{s-1}{\gamma}-\frac{4}{\eta}\big)\|z^{k}-x^{\star}\|^{2}\geq\frac{A_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2},\end{array}$
(62)
where we have used $\gamma:=\frac{(s-2)\eta}{32(s-1)}$ to evaluate $\frac{s^{2}}{2}\big(\frac{s-1}{\gamma}-\frac{4}{\eta}\big)=\frac{2s^{2}(8s^{2}-17s+10)}{(s-2)\eta}>0$ for all $s>2$ in the last inequality.
Moreover, using $\rho_{*}\leq\rho_{n}$ , $2\rho_{n}\leq\frac{s-2}{4(s-1)}\eta$ from (19), and $\gamma t_{k-1}\leq\frac{\gamma}{2}t_{k-1}^{2}$ due to $t_{k-1}\geq s>2$ , we also have
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}A_{k}&:=&\big[\frac{3(3s-2)}{16(s-1)}\eta-2\rho_{n}\big]t_{k-1}^{2}+2s(\rho_{n}-\rho_{*})t_{k-1}-\gamma t_{k-1}+\gamma\\
&\geq&\big[\frac{3(3s-2)}{16(s-1)}\eta-2\rho_{n}-\frac{\gamma}{2}\big]t_{k-1}^{2}\geq\big[\frac{3(3s-2)}{16(s-1)}-\frac{s-2}{4(s-1)}-\frac{(s-2)}{64(s-1)}\big]\eta(k+s)^{2}\\
&=&\frac{(19s+10)\eta}{64(s-1)}(k+s)^{2}.\end{array}$
Therefore, we obtain
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}\big[\|w^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\frac{128(s-1)(\mathcal{L}_{0}+\Lambda_{0}\eta\mathcal{S}_{k})}{(19s+10)\eta(k+s)^{2}}=\frac{128(s-1)(\mathcal{R}_{0}^{2}+\Lambda_{0}\mathcal{S}_{k})}{(19s+10)(k+s)^{2}},\end{array}$
which is the first bound of (22), where $\mathcal{R}_{0}$ is defined in (21).
Next, since
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}A_{k}&=&\frac{3(3s-2)}{16(s-1)}\eta t_{k-1}^{2}-2\rho_{n}t_{k-1}(t_{k-1}-s)-2s\rho_{*}t_{k-1}-\gamma(t_{k-1}-1)\leq\frac{3(3s-2)}{16(s-1)}\eta t_{k-1}^{2},\end{array}$
(63)
from (18), we have
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\omega&=&\frac{\varphi}{4}+\frac{2\Lambda_{0}\Theta(s+1)^{2}}{s^{2}}+\frac{s-2}{16(s-1)}\geq\frac{\varphi}{4}\geq\frac{9(85s-134)}{256(s-1)}\geq\frac{3(3s-2)}{16(s-1)}\geq\frac{A_{k}}{\eta t_{k-1}^{2}}.\end{array}$
By the $L$ -Lipschitz continuity of $G$ and Young’s inequality, we have
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\frac{A_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{\omega\eta L^{2}t_{k-1}^{2}}{2}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}&\geq&\frac{A_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{\omega\eta t_{k-1}^{2}}{2}\|Gx^{k}-Gy^{k-1}\|^{2}\\
&=&\frac{A_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{\omega\eta t_{k-1}^{2}}{2}\|w^{k}-\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}\\
&\geq&\frac{A_{k}}{4}\|\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}\\
&\geq&\frac{(19s+10)\eta}{256(s-1)}(k+s)^{2}\|\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}.\end{array}$
Combining this inequality, (54), and (62), we can show that
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{L}_{k}&\geq&\mathcal{P}_{k}+\frac{\omega\eta L^{2}t_{k-1}^{2}}{2}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}\geq\frac{(19s+10)\eta}{256(s-1)}(k+s)^{2}\|\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}.\end{array}$
Hence, utilizing this inequality, with similar arguments as the proof of the first line of (22), we obtain the second line of (22) from this expression.
Step 3 (Other intermediate summability results).
Next, since $\epsilon_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:=1-(s+1)M^{2}\eta^{2}}>0$ (due to the choice $\eta<{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{\lambda}{L}}$ as in (19)) and $\mathcal{S}_{\infty}:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\delta_{k}<+\infty$ , we can rearrange (60) and then use Lemma A.1 to prove that $\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{L}_{k}\big]$ exists and
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}\mathbb{E}\big[\|w^{k}\|^{2}\big]}&<&+\infty,\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}\mathbb{E}\big[\|\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}\big]}&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|x^{k+1}-y^{k}\|^{2}\big]&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}(t_{k}-s)\mathbb{E}\big[\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-w^{k}\|^{2}\big]&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\Delta_{k}\big]&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|e^{k}\|^{2}\big]&<&+\infty.\end{array}$
(64)
By Assumption 1 and Young’s inequality, we easily get
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\|w^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}&\leq&L^{2}\|x^{k+1}-y^{k}\|^{2},\\
\|w^{k+1}-w^{k}\|^{2}&\leq&2\|w^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}+2\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-w^{k}\|^{2},\\
\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}&\leq&2\|w^{k}-\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}+2\|w^{k}-\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}.\end{array}$
Moreover, we also have $\mathbb{E}\big[\|\hat{e}^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq 2\mathbb{E}\big[\|e^{k-1}\|^{2}\big]+2\mathbb{E}\big[\|w^{k}-\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}\big]$ .
Using these relations and $t_{k}(t_{k}-s)\sim t_{k}^{2}$ , we can derive from (64) that
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|w^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}\big]&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|w^{k+1}-w^{k}\|^{2}\big]&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}\big]&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|\hat{e}^{k}\|^{2}\big]&<&+\infty.\end{array}$
(65)
Step 4 (Applying Lemmas 5.6 and 5.7).
First, from (64) and (65), we see that the conditions (56) of Lemma 5.6 are fulfilled.
Next, from (60), by induction, we get $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{L}_{k}\big]\leq\mathcal{L}_{0}+\eta\Lambda_{0}\mathcal{S}_{\infty}$ for all $k\geq 0$ .
Moreover, by (62), we have $\mathcal{P}_{k}\leq\mathcal{L}_{k}$ .
Therefore, we conclude that $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{P}_{k}\big]\leq\mathcal{L}_{0}+\eta\Lambda_{0}\mathcal{S}_{\infty}\equiv\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{0}$ .
Next, by (54), we have
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{L}_{k}&:=&\mathcal{P}_{k}+\frac{\omega\eta L^{2}t_{k-1}^{2}}{2}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}\eta(1-\kappa)(s+1)^{2}t_{k-1}^{2}}{s^{2}}\Delta_{k-1}+\big[\varphi+\frac{s-2}{32(s-1)}\big]\eta t_{k-1}^{2}\|e^{k-1}\|^{2}.\end{array}$
Since the last three terms are summable due to (64), their limit is zero.
Using these facts and the existence of $\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{L}_{k}\big]$ proved above, we conclude that $\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{P}_{k}\big]$ also exists.
From the first line of (22), we also say that $\{t_{k-1}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|w^{k}\|^{2}\big]\}$ is bounded, and from the first line of (20), we have $\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k-1}\mathbb{E}\big[\|w^{k}\|^{2}\big]<+\infty$ .
Overall, we have verified all the conditions of Lemma 5.7.
Hence, we obtain (23) from (59).
Step 5 (Iteration-complexity).
For a given tolerance $\epsilon>0$ , from (22) and $\mathcal{S}_{k}\leq\mathcal{S}_{\infty}<+\infty$ , to guarantee $\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}$ , we need to impose ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{C_{0}^{2}(\mathcal{R}_{0}^{2}+\Lambda_{0}S_{\infty})}{(k+s)^{2}}}\leq\epsilon^{2}$ .
This relation implies that $k=\mathcal{O}\big(1/\epsilon\big)$ , which proves our statement (d).
$\square$
5.2 The proof of Theorem 3.2: Almost sure convergence.
Similar to the proof of Theorem 3.1, we also divide this proof into several steps as follows.
Step 1 (Almost sure summability bounds).
For $\mathcal{L}_{k}$ given in (54), from (55), we have
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{L}_{k}-\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\mathcal{L}_{k+1}\big]&\geq&\frac{\epsilon_{1}\eta t_{k}(t_{k}-s)}{2}\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-w^{k}\|^{2}\big]+\frac{\eta st_{k}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}\big]\\
&&+{~}\frac{(s-2)(4s-7)\eta(k+1)}{64(s-1)}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{(s-2)\eta L^{2}t_{k-1}^{2}}{32(s-1)}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}\\
&&+{~}\frac{(2s+1)\Lambda_{0}\eta{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(1-\kappa)}t_{k-1}(t_{k-1}-s)}{s^{2}}\Delta_{k-1}+\frac{(s-2)\eta t_{k-1}^{2}}{32(s-1)}\|e^{k-1}\|^{2}-\Lambda_{0}\eta\delta_{k}.\end{array}$
(66)
Let us define
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}U_{k}&:=&\mathcal{L}_{k}+\frac{\epsilon_{1}\eta t_{k-1}(t_{k-1}-s)}{2}\|\hat{w}^{k}-w^{k-1}\|^{2}+\frac{\eta st_{k-1}}{2}\|\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}.\end{array}$
(67)
Then, we can rearrange (66) and use $U_{k}$ from (67) to get
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[U_{k+1}\big]&\leq&U_{k}-\frac{\epsilon_{1}\eta t_{k-1}(t_{k-1}-s)}{2}\|\hat{w}^{k}-w^{k-1}\|^{2}-\frac{\eta st_{k-1}}{2}\|\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}\\
&&-{~}\frac{(s-2)(4s-7)\eta(k+1)}{64(s-1)}\|w^{k}\|^{2}-\frac{(2s+1)\Lambda_{0}\eta{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(1-\kappa)}t_{k-1}(t_{k-1}-s)}{s^{2}}\Delta_{k-1}\\
&&-{~}\frac{(s-2)\eta L^{2}t_{k-1}^{2}}{32(s-1)}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}-\frac{(s-2)\eta t_{k-1}^{2}}{32(s-1)}\|e^{k-1}\|^{2}+\Lambda_{0}\eta\delta_{k}.\end{array}$
(68)
Note that $\mathcal{L}_{k}\geq 0$ almost surely for all $k\geq 0$ and $\mathcal{S}_{\infty}:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\delta_{k}<+\infty$ by our assumption, applying Lemma A.3 to the last inequality, we conclude that $\lim_{k\to\infty}U_{k}$ exists almost surely.
Moreover, we also have the following almost sure summability bounds:
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+s)\|\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+1)\|w^{k}\|^{2}&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+s+1)^{2}\|e^{k}\|^{2}&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+1)(k+s+1)\Delta_{k}&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+1)(k+s+1)\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-w^{k}\|^{2}&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+s)^{2}\|x^{k+1}-y^{k}\|^{2}&<&+\infty.\end{array}$
(69)
The first two lines of (69) are exactly (24).
Similar to the proof of (65), but using (69), we can also show that
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}^{2}\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}&<&+\infty\quad\textrm{almost surely},\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}^{2}\|w^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}&<&+\infty\quad\textrm{almost surely}.\end{array}$
(70)
From (69) we also have $\lim_{k\to\infty}t_{k-1}(t_{k-1}-s)\|\hat{w}^{k}-w^{k-1}\|^{2}=0$ and $\lim_{k\to\infty}t_{k-1}\|\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}=0$ almost surely.
Combining these facts and the existence of $\lim_{k\to\infty}U_{k}$ almost surely, (67) implies that $\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{L}_{k}$ also exists almost surely.
Step 2 (The bounds and almost sure convergence of $u^{k}:=s(z^{k}-x^{k})$ ).
Let $u^{k}:=s(z^{k}-x^{k})$ and $\hat{u}^{k}:=s(z^{k}-x^{k})-s\eta\hat{w}^{k}$ .
Then, similar to the proof of Lemma 5.6, we can show that
${\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hat{u}^{k+1}&=&\big(1-\frac{s}{t_{k}}\big)\hat{u}^{k}+\frac{s}{t_{k}}\cdot\frac{t_{k}}{s}\big[[s(\eta-\beta_{k})-\gamma_{k}-\frac{s^{2}\eta}{t_{k}}]\hat{w}^{k}+s\eta e^{k}-(s\beta_{k}+\gamma_{k})e^{k-1}\big].\end{array}}\vskip-2.15277pt$
Since $\frac{s}{t_{k}}\in(0,1)$ , by convexity of $\|\cdot\|^{2}$ , Young’s inequality, $\beta_{k}\leq\eta$ , and $\gamma_{k}\leq\eta$ , we obtain the following inequality after taking the conditional expectation $\mathbb{E}_{k}\left[\cdot\right]$ :
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}_{k}\left[\|\hat{u}^{k+1}\|^{2}\right]&\leq&\big(1-\frac{s}{t_{k}}\big)\|\hat{u}^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{3t_{k}}{s}[s(\eta-\beta_{k})-\gamma_{k}-\frac{s^{2}\eta}{t_{k}}]^{2}\|\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}\\
&&+{~}3s\eta^{2}t_{k}\mathbb{E}_{k}\left[\|e^{k}\|^{2}\right]+\frac{3t_{k}}{s}(s\beta_{k}+\gamma_{k})^{2}\|e^{k-1}\|^{2}\\
&\leq&\big(1-\frac{s}{t_{k}}\big)\|\hat{u}^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{12}{s}\big[(2s^{2}+1)+\frac{s^{4}}{t_{k}^{2}}\big]\eta^{2}t_{k}\|\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}\\
&&+{~}3s\eta^{2}t_{k}\mathbb{E}_{k}\left[\|e^{k}\|^{2}\right]+\frac{3(s+1)^{2}\eta^{2}t_{k}}{s}\|e^{k-1}\|^{2}.\end{array}$
By (15) and $1-\kappa\leq 1$ , we have
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|e^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\Delta_{k}\big]&\leq&\Delta_{k-1}+\Theta L^{2}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}+\frac{\delta_{k}}{t_{k}^{2}}.\end{array}$
Combining the last two inequalities, and noting that $\kappa\in(0,1]$ , we finally get
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}_{k}\left[\|\hat{u}^{k+1}\|^{2}\right]&\leq&\|\hat{u}^{k}\|^{2}-\frac{s}{t_{k}}\|\hat{u}^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{12}{s}\big[(2s^{2}+1)\eta^{2}t_{k}+\frac{s^{4}\eta}{t_{k}}\big]\|\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{3(s+1)^{2}\eta^{2}t_{k}}{s}\|e^{k-1}\|^{2}\\
&&+{~}3s\eta^{2}t_{k}\Delta_{k-1}+3s\eta^{2}L^{2}\Theta t_{k}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}+\frac{3s\eta^{2}\delta_{k}}{t_{k}}.\end{array}$
(71)
Note that the third to the sixth terms of this expression are almost surely summable due to (69) and (70).
Moreover, we also have $\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\delta_{k}}{t_{k}}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\delta_{k}<+\infty$ .
Applying again Lemma A.3 to the last inequality and using these facts, we conclude that almost surely
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\lim_{k\to\infty}\|z^{k}-x^{k}-\eta\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}\ \ \textrm{exists \quad and \quad}\ \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\|z^{k}-x^{k}-\eta\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}}{t_{k}}<+\infty.\end{array}$
(72)
In addition, we have $\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t_{k}}=+\infty$ .
Utilizing this fact and (72), by a contradiction argument, we must have $\lim_{k\to\infty}\|z^{k}-x^{k}-\eta\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}=0$ almost surely.
Moreover, from (69), it follows that
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\lim_{k\to+\infty}(k+s)\|\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}=0\quad\text{almost surely}\end{array}$
By Young’s inequality, we have
$\|s(z^{k}-x^{k})\|^{2}\leq 2s^{2}\|z^{k}-x^{k}-\eta\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}+2s^{2}\eta^{2}\|\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}.$
Combining the last three facts and (72), and using $t_{k}=k+s+1$ , we conclude that
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\lim_{k\to\infty}\|z^{k}-x^{k}\|^{2}=0\textrm{\quad and \quad}\ \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\|z^{k}-x^{k}\|^{2}}{t_{k}}<+\infty.\end{array}$
(73)
Step 3 (The proof of (25)).
First, from (45) we have $\hat{e}^{k}=\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}-Gx^{k}=e^{k-1}+Gy^{k-1}-Gx^{k}$ .
By Young’s inequality and (3) in Assumption 1, the last expression leads to
$\|\hat{e}^{k}\|^{2}\leq 2\|e^{k-1}\|^{2}+2\|Gx^{k}-Gy^{k-1}\|^{2}\leq 2\|e^{k-1}\|^{2}+2L^{2}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}$ .
Exploiting this inequality and $\langle w^{k},x^{k}-x^{\star}\rangle\geq-\rho_{n}\|w^{k}\|^{2}$ for any $x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)$ from (6) of Assumption 1, similar to the proof of (100), we can show that
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|z^{k+1}-x^{\star}\|^{2}\big]&\leq&\big(1+\frac{\eta}{st_{k}^{2}}\big)\|z^{k}-x^{\star}\|^{2}+\big(\frac{\eta t_{k}}{s}+\frac{2\eta\rho_{n}}{s}+\frac{2\eta^{2}}{s^{2}}\big)\|w^{k}\|^{2}\\
&&+{~}\frac{\eta}{st_{k}}\|z^{k}-x^{k}\|^{2}+\big(\frac{\eta t_{k}^{2}}{s}+\frac{2\eta^{2}}{s^{2}}\big)[2\|e^{k-1}\|^{2}+2L^{2}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}].\end{array}$
(74)
Since $\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\eta}{st_{k}^{2}}<+\infty$ and the last three terms of (74) are almost surely summable due to $t_{k}=k+s~+~1$ , (69), and (73),
applying Lemma A.3 to (74), we conclude that $\lim_{k\to\infty}\|z^{k}-x^{\star}\|^{2}$ exists almost surely.
Next, from (54), we have
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{L}_{k}&:=&\mathcal{P}_{k}+\frac{\omega\eta L^{2}t_{k-1}^{2}}{2}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}\eta(1-\kappa)(s+1)^{2}t_{k-1}^{2}}{s^{2}}\Delta_{k-1}+\big(\varphi+\frac{s-2}{32(s-1)}\big)\eta t_{k-1}^{2}\|e^{k-1}\|^{2}.\end{array}$
However, the last three terms are almost surely summable due to (69), their limit is zero.
Hence, we conclude that $\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{P}_{k}$ also exists almost surely.
Since $\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{P}_{k}$ exists almost surely, and $\mathcal{P}_{k}\geq\frac{A_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}\geq\frac{C_{1}t_{k-1}^{2}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}$ due to the proof of Theorem 3.1, where $C_{1}:=\frac{(19s+10)\eta}{64(s-1)}$ , there exists $\bar{M}>0$ such that $t_{k-1}^{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}\leq\bar{M}^{2}$ almost surely.
Using this inequality and $\lim_{k\to\infty}\|x^{k}-z^{k}\|=0$ almost surely, we can show that almost surely
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}t_{k-1}|\langle w^{k},x^{k}-z^{k}\rangle|&\leq&t_{k-1}\|w^{k}\|\|x^{k}-z^{k}\|\leq\bar{M}\|x^{k}-z^{k}\|\to 0\quad\text{as}~k\to\infty.\end{array}$
This expression implies that $\lim_{k\to\infty}t_{k-1}\langle w^{k},x^{k}-z^{k}\rangle=0$ almost surely.
Now, recall $\mathcal{P}_{k}$ from (47) as
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{P}_{k}=\frac{a_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+st_{k-1}\langle w^{k},x^{k}-z^{k}\rangle+\frac{s^{2}(s-1)}{2\gamma_{k-1}}\|z^{k}-x^{\star}\|^{2}.\end{array}$
Since $\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{P}_{k}$ and $\lim_{k\to\infty}\|z^{k}-x^{\star}\|^{2}$ exist almost surely, $\lim_{k\to\infty}t_{k-1}\langle w^{k},x^{k}-z^{k}\rangle=0$ almost surely, $\lim_{k\to\infty}\gamma_{k-1}=\gamma>0$ , and $\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{t_{k-1}^{2}}{a_{k}}=\frac{1}{\psi}$ , we conclude that $\lim_{k\to\infty}t_{k-1}^{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}$ exists almost surely.
However, since $\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k-1}\|w^{k}\|^{2}<+\infty$ due to (69), applying Lemma A.2, we can easily argue that $\lim_{k\to\infty}t_{k-1}^{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}=0$ almost surely.
This limit implies the first limit of (25).
Note that
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}t_{k-1}^{2}\|\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}&\leq&2t_{k-1}^{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+2t_{k-1}^{2}\|Gx^{k}-Gy^{k-1}\|^{2}\leq 2t_{k-1}^{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+2L^{2}t_{k-1}^{2}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}.\end{array}$
Since $\lim_{k\to\infty}t_{k-1}^{2}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}=0$ and $\lim_{k\to\infty}t_{k-1}^{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}=0$ almost surely, the last inequality implies that $\lim_{k\to\infty}t_{k-1}^{2}\|\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}=0$ , which leads to the second limit of (25).
Step 4 (The almost sure convergence of iterates).
Note that $|\|x^{k}-x^{\star}\|-\|z^{k}-x^{\star}\||\leq\|x^{k}-z^{k}\|$ .
Since $\lim_{k\to\infty}\|x^{k}-z^{k}\|=0$ almost surely and $\lim_{k\to\infty}\|z^{k}-x^{\star}\|$ exists almost surely, we conclude that $\lim_{k\to\infty}\|x^{k}-x^{\star}\|$ exists almost surely.
Let $\mathcal{D}=\{x_{1}^{\star},x_{2}^{\star},\cdots\}$ be a countable dense set in $\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)$ .
For each $x_{j}^{\star}\in\mathcal{D}$ , we have proven $\mathbb{P}\big(\lim_{k\to\infty}\|x^{k}-x^{\star}_{j}\|\ \text{exists}\big)=1$ .
If we define $\Omega_{j}:=\{\omega:\lim_{k\to\infty}\|x^{k}(\omega)-x^{\star}_{j}\|\ \text{exists}\}$ , then $\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{j})=1$ .
Since $\mathcal{D}$ is countable, $\Omega:=\bigcap_{j=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{j}$ also satisfies $\mathbb{P}(\Omega)=1$ .
Now, fix $\omega\in\Omega$ .
For every $\hat{x}^{\star}\in\mathcal{D}$ , $\lim_{k\to\infty}\|x^{k}(\omega)-\hat{x}^{\star}\|$ exists.
For every $x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)$ and $\hat{x}^{\star}\in\mathcal{D}$ , we have $|\|x^{k}-x^{\star}\|-\|x^{k}-\hat{x}^{\star}\||\leq\|x^{\star}-\hat{x}^{\star}\|$ .
Therefore, for any $m,n\geq 0$ , we have
$|\|x^{m}-x^{\star}\|-\|x^{n}-x^{\star}\||\leq|\|x^{m}-\hat{x}^{\star}\|-\|x^{n}-\hat{x}^{\star}\||+2\|x^{\star}-\hat{x}^{\star}\|.$
For any $\epsilon>0$ , since $\mathcal{D}$ is dense in $\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)$ , we can choose $\hat{x}^{\star}\in\mathcal{D}$ such that $\|x^{\star}-\hat{x}^{\star}\|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4}$ .
On the other hand, since $\left\{\|x^{k}-\hat{x}^{\star}\|\right\}$ converges, it is Cauchy.
Consequently, for sufficiently large $m$ and $n$ , we have $|\|x^{m}-\hat{x}^{\star}\|-\|x^{n}-\hat{x}^{\star}\||\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}$ .
Using these facts into the last inequality, we get $|\|x^{m}-x^{\star}\|-\|x^{n}-x^{\star}\||\leq\epsilon$ .
Thus, $\lim_{k\to\infty}\|x^{k}-x^{\star}\|$ exists for every $x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)$ .
From the second line of (69), we have $\lim_{k\to\infty}\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|=0$ almost surely, for $v^{k}\in Tx^{k}$ .
This means that $\lim_{k\to\infty}\|w^{k}\|=0$ almost surely for $(x^{k},w^{k})\in\mathrm{gra}(\Phi)$ .
Moreover, $\mathrm{gra}(\Phi)$ is closed by our assumption.
Applying Lemma A.4 from the appendix, we conclude that $\{x^{k}\}$ converges almost surely to a $\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)$ -valued random variable $x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)$ .
Finally, since $\lim_{k\to\infty}\|x^{k}-z^{k}\|=0$ almost surely and, by the last line of (69), $\lim_{k\to\infty}\|x^{k+1}-y^{k}\|=0$ almost surely, we can also conclude that both $\{z^{k}\}$ and $\{y^{k}\}$ also converge almost surely to the same $x^{\star}$ .
$\square$
5.3 The full proof of Theorem 4.5.
Before presenting the full proof of Theorem 4.5, we need the following supporting lemma, whose proof is given in Appendix C.4.
Lemma 5.8
Let $\hat{\omega}$ , $\hat{\lambda}$ , and $\hat{\mu}$ be defined by (30).
For (GE), suppose that $G$ satisfies Assumptions 1 and 1 with $\alpha\in[0,1)$ and $\rho_{c}>0$ such that $L\rho_{n}\leq\hat{\mu}$ .
Let $\{(x^{k},y^{k},z^{k})\}$ be generated by (VAPEG) using a variance-reduced estimator $\widetilde{G}y^{k}$ for $Gy^{k}$ satisfying Definition 4.1.
For a given $s>2$ , let $\varphi_{s}:=\frac{9(85s-134)}{64(s-1)}+\frac{9(s-2)}{64}$ and suppose that $\eta$ , $\beta_{k}$ , and $\gamma_{k}$ are respectively updated by
$\hskip-12.91663pt\begin{array}[]{ll}&\frac{8(s-1)\rho_{n}}{s-2}\leq\eta\leq\frac{\hat{\lambda}}{L},\quad\gamma_{k}:=\frac{(s-2)\eta(k+s)}{32(s-1)(k+s+1)},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\beta_{k}:=\big[\frac{3(s-2)\eta}{16(s-1)}+2\rho_{n}]\frac{k+1}{k+s+1}-\frac{\gamma_{k}}{k+s+1}.\end{array}\hskip-12.91663pt$
(75)
Suppose further that there exist $\bar{c}\in(0,1)$ and a sufficiently small $\epsilon_{2}\geq 0$ such that for every $k\geq 0$ , the parameters $\kappa_{k}$ and $\Theta_{k}$ in (27) of Definition 4.1 satisfy the following condition:
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\big[\varphi_{s}+\frac{25s-34}{2(s-2)}\big]\frac{3\eta\Theta_{k}t_{k}}{\rho_{c}(t_{k}-s)}\leq\bar{c}\leq\frac{1}{1+\epsilon_{2}}\Big[1-\frac{(1-\kappa_{k})t_{k}^{2}}{t_{k-1}^{2}}\Big].\end{array}$
(76)
For $\bar{c}$ in (76) and $a_{k}$ in Lemma 5.2, define the following Lyapunov function:
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}&:=&\frac{a_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+st_{k-1}\langle w^{k},x^{k}-z^{k}\rangle+\frac{s^{2}(s-1)}{2\gamma_{k-1}}\|z^{k}-x^{\star}\|^{2}+\rho_{c}t_{k-1}^{2}|\!\|w^{k}-\hat{w}^{k}|\!\|^{2}\\
&&+{~}\frac{\varphi_{s}\eta L^{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t_{k-1}^{2}}}{8}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}+\big[\varphi_{s}+\frac{25s-34}{2(s-2)}\big]\frac{(1-\bar{c})\eta t_{k-1}^{2}}{\bar{c}}\Delta_{k-1}+\varphi_{s}\eta t_{k-1}^{2}\|e^{k-1}\|^{2}.\end{array}$
(77)
Then, the following inequality holds:
$\hskip 0.0pt\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}-\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}\big]&\geq&\frac{\eta s(k+s+1)}{2}\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}\big]+\frac{\phi_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+s(s-1)\langle w^{k},x^{k}-x^{\star}\rangle\\
&&+{~}\frac{\eta t_{k}(t_{k}-s)}{2}\big(1-\frac{M^{2}\eta^{2}t_{k}}{t_{k}-s}\big)\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-w^{k}\|^{2}\big]\\
&&+{~}\frac{2\alpha\rho_{c}t_{k}^{2}}{1-\alpha}\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|w^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}\big]+\frac{(s-2)\eta L^{2}t_{k}^{2}}{32(s-1)}\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|x^{k+1}-y^{k}\|^{2}\big]\\
&&+{~}\big[\varphi_{s}+\frac{25s-34}{2(s-2)}\big]\epsilon_{2}\eta t_{k-1}^{2}\Delta_{k-1}-\big[\varphi_{s}+\frac{25s-34}{2(s-2)}\big]\frac{\eta\delta_{k}}{\bar{c}},\end{array}\hskip 0.0pt$
(78)
where $\phi_{k}:=\big[\frac{(20s-23)(s-2)}{32(s-1)}\eta-4(s-1)\rho_{n}\big](k+s)+(s-1)\big[\frac{(3s+10)\eta}{16}+2(2s-1)\rho_{n}\big]+\frac{(s-2)\eta}{32(s-1)}$ .
The proof of Theorem 4.5.
For the sake of clarity, we divide this proof into several steps.
Step 1 (Verifying condition (32)).
First, the update rules of parameters in (31) come from (75) of Lemma 5.8.
Next, we verify the condition (76) of Lemma 5.8.
Since we can choose $\epsilon_{2}$ sufficiently small, (76) holds if
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\big[\varphi_{s}+\frac{25s-34}{2(s-2)}\big]\frac{3\eta\Theta_{k}t_{k}}{\rho_{c}(t_{k}-s)}<1-\frac{(1-\kappa_{k})t_{k}^{2}}{t_{k-1}^{2}}.\end{array}$
If we denote $\hat{C}:=3\big[\varphi_{s}+\frac{25s-34}{2(s-2)}\big]$ , then since $t_{k}=k+s+1$ , this condition becomes
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\frac{\hat{C}\eta\Theta_{k}(k+s+1)}{\rho_{c}(k+1)}<1-\frac{(1-\kappa_{k})(k+s+1)^{2}}{(k+s)^{2}}=\frac{\kappa_{k}(k+s+1)^{2}}{(k+s)^{2}}-\frac{2(k+s)+1}{(k+s)^{2}}.\end{array}$
Because $k\geq 0$ and $2(k+s)+1<2(k+s+1)$ , this condition holds if
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\kappa_{k}\geq\frac{s^{2}\hat{C}\eta\Theta_{k}}{(s+1)\rho_{c}}+\frac{2}{k+s+1}=\frac{\Gamma\eta\Theta_{k}}{\rho_{c}}+\frac{2}{k+s+1},\end{array}$
where $\Gamma:=\frac{3s^{2}}{s+1}\big[\varphi_{s}+\frac{25s-34}{2(s-2)}\big]$ is given in (30).
This condition is exactly (32).
Note that if there exist $\underline{\kappa}$ and $\Theta>0$ such that $0<\underline{\kappa}\leq\kappa_{k}<1$ and $0<\Theta_{k}\leq\Theta$ , then under the condition (32), we can easily show that there exists $\epsilon_{2}>0$ such that
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\frac{\rho_{c}(t_{k}-s)}{3\big[\varphi_{s}+\frac{25s-34}{2(s-2)}\big]\eta\Theta_{k}t_{k}}\big[1-\frac{(1-\kappa_{k})t_{k}^{2}}{t_{k-1}^{2}}\big]-1\geq\epsilon_{2}>0.\end{array}$
Step 2 (The summability results in (35)).
By (5) in Assumption 1, we have $\langle w^{k},x^{k}-x^{\star}\rangle\geq-\rho_{*}\|w^{k}\|^{2}$ .
Moreover, similar to the proof of Lemma 5.5, we also have
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\phi_{k}-2\rho_{*}s(s-1)&=&\big[\frac{(20s-23)(s-2)}{32(s-1)}\eta-4(s-1)\rho_{n}\big](k+s)+\frac{(s-1)(3s+10)\eta}{16}\\
&&+{~}2(2s-1)(s-1)\rho_{n}+\frac{(s-2)\eta}{32(s-1)}-2\rho_{*}s(s-1)\\
&\geq&\frac{(s-2)(4s-7)\eta(k+1)}{32(s-1)}>0.\end{array}$
Substituting these facts into (78) in Lemma 5.8, we get
$\hskip-8.61108pt\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}-\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}\big]&\geq&\frac{\eta s(k+s+1)}{2}\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}\big]+\frac{(s-2)(4s-7)\eta(k+1)}{64(s-1)}\|w^{k}\|^{2}\\
&&+{~}\frac{\eta t_{k}(t_{k}-s)}{2}\big(1-\frac{M^{2}\eta^{2}t_{k}}{t_{k}-s}\big)\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-w^{k}\|^{2}\big]+\frac{(s-2)\eta L^{2}t_{k}^{2}}{32(s-1)}\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|x^{k+1}-y^{k}\|^{2}\big]\\
&&+{~}\frac{2\alpha\rho_{c}t_{k}^{2}}{1-\alpha}\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|w^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}\big]+\big[\varphi_{s}+\frac{25s-34}{2(s-2)}\big]\epsilon_{2}\eta t_{k-1}^{2}\Delta_{k-1}-\big[\varphi_{s}+\frac{25s-34}{2(s-2)}\big]\frac{\eta\delta_{k}}{\bar{c}}.\end{array}\hskip-17.22217pt$
(79)
Now, taking the full expectation $\mathbb{E}\big[\cdot\big]$ on both sides of (79), we obtain
$\hskip-12.91663pt\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}\big]&\geq&\frac{\eta s(k+s+1)}{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}\big]+\frac{(s-2)(4s-7)\eta(k+1)}{64(s-1)}\mathbb{E}\big[\|w^{k}\|^{2}\big]\\
&&+{~}\frac{(s-2)\eta L^{2}t_{k}^{2}}{32(s-1)}\mathbb{E}\big[\|x^{k+1}-y^{k}\|^{2}\big]+\big[\varphi_{s}+\frac{25s-34}{2(s-2)}\big]\epsilon_{2}\eta t_{k-1}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\Delta_{k-1}\big]\\
&&+{~}\frac{\eta t_{k}(t_{k}-s)}{2}\big(1-\frac{M^{2}\eta^{2}t_{k}}{t_{k}-s}\big)\mathbb{E}\big[\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-w^{k}\|^{2}\big]\\
&&+{~}\frac{2\alpha\rho_{c}t_{k}^{2}}{1-\alpha}\mathbb{E}\big[\|w^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}\big]-\big[\varphi_{s}+\frac{25s-34}{2(s-2)}\big]\frac{\eta\delta_{k}}{\bar{c}}.\end{array}\hskip-25.83325pt$
(80)
Summing (80) from $k=0$ to $K$ and noting that $\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\geq 0$ due to (77) and Lemma 5.4, we have
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\eta s(k+s+1)\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}\right]&\leq&2(\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\hat{\Lambda}_{0}\eta\mathcal{S}_{K}}),\\
\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{(s-2)(4s-7)\eta}{s-1}(k+1)\mathbb{E}\left[\|w^{k}\|^{2}\right]&\leq&64(\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\hat{\Lambda}_{0}\eta\mathcal{S}_{K}}),\end{array}$
(81)
where $\hat{\Lambda}_{0}$ is defined in (30) and $\mathcal{S}_{K}:=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\delta_{k}$ .
Here, we have used the fact that $\bar{c}\geq\frac{24(s^{2}-1)\rho_{n}\underline{\Theta}}{(s-2)\rho_{c}}\big[\varphi_{s}+\frac{25s-34}{2(s-2)}\big]$ derived from (76), leading to $\hat{\Lambda}_{0}$ as in (30).
Finally, since $z^{0}=x^{0}=y^{-1}$ and the conventions $e^{-1}=0$ , and $\Delta_{-1}=0$ , we have from (77) that
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{0}&=&\frac{a_{0}}{2}\|w^{0}\|^{2}+\frac{s^{2}(s-1)}{2\gamma_{-1}}\|x^{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}=\frac{[2(13s-10)s^{2}-(s-1)(s-2)]\eta}{64(s-1)}\|w^{0}\|^{2}+\frac{16s^{3}(s-1)}{(s-2)\eta}\|x^{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}.\end{array}$
Here, we have used $a_{0}=[\frac{(13s-10)\eta}{16(s-1)}-2\rho_{n}]s^{2}+2s^{2}\rho_{n}-\frac{(s-2)\eta}{32}=\frac{[2(13s-10)s^{2}-(s-1)(s-2)]\eta}{32(s-1)}>0$ for all $s>2$ and $\gamma_{-1}=\frac{(s-2)\eta(t_{-1}-1)}{32(s-1)t_{-1}}=\frac{{(s-2)\eta}}{32s}$ .
Substituting $\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{0}$ into (81), we obtain (35).
Step 3 (The proof of the bounds in (33)).
Now, from (80), by induction, we also have ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\big]\leq\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{0}+\hat{\Lambda}_{0}\eta\mathcal{S}_{k}}$ .
However, from (77) and (53), we also have
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{\mathcal{L}}}_{k}\geq\mathcal{P}_{k}&\geq&\frac{A_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{s^{2}}{2}\big(\frac{s-1}{\gamma}-\frac{4}{\eta}\big)\|z^{k}-x^{\star}\|^{2}\geq\frac{A_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2},\end{array}$
(82)
where, similarly to the proof of Theorem 3.1,
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}A_{k}&:=&\big[\frac{3(3s-2)\eta}{16(s-1)}-2\rho_{n}\big]t_{k-1}^{2}+2s(\rho_{n}-\rho_{*})t_{k-1}-\gamma t_{k-1}+\gamma\geq\frac{(19s+10)\eta}{64(s-1)}(k+s)^{2},\end{array}$
Therefore, we obtain
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}\big[\|w^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\frac{128(s-1)(\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{0}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{\Lambda}_{0}\eta\mathcal{S}_{k}})}{(19s+10)\eta(k+s)^{2}}=\frac{128(s-1)(\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{\Lambda}_{0}\mathcal{S}_{k}})}{(19s+10)(k+s)^{2}},\end{array}$
which is the first bound of (33), where
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}&:=&\frac{2(13s-10)s^{2}-(s-1)(s-2)}{64(s-1)}\|w^{0}\|^{2}+\frac{16s^{3}(s-1)}{(s-2)\eta^{2}}\|x^{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}.\end{array}$
From (77), using
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\frac{\varphi_{s}\eta}{4}+2\rho_{c}\geq\frac{\varphi_{s}\eta}{4}\geq\frac{9(85s-134)\eta}{256(s-1)}\geq\frac{3(3s-2)\eta}{16(s-1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:SFOG_th31_proof2.1}}}{{\geq}}\frac{A_{k}}{t_{k-1}^{2}},\end{array}$
the $L$ -Lipschitz continuity of $G$ , the relation $|\!\|\cdot|\!\|\geq\|\cdot\|$ , and Young’s inequality, we have
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{T}_{[1]}&:=&\frac{A_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{\varphi_{s}\eta L^{2}t_{k-1}^{2}}{8}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}+\rho_{c}t_{k-1}^{2}|\!\|w^{k}-\hat{w}^{k}|\!\|^{2}\\
&\geq&\frac{A_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+\big(\frac{\varphi_{s}\eta}{8}+\rho_{c}\big)t_{k-1}^{2}\|w^{k}-\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}\\
&\geq&\frac{A_{k}}{4}\|\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}\\
&\geq&\frac{(19s+10)\eta}{256(s-1)}(k+s)^{2}\|\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}.\end{array}$
Combining this inequality, (53), and (77), we can show that
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}&\geq&\mathcal{P}_{k}+\frac{\varphi_{s}\eta L^{2}t_{k-1}^{2}}{8}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}+\rho_{c}t_{k-1}^{2}|\!\|w^{k}-\hat{w}^{k}|\!\|^{2}\\
&\geq&\frac{(19s+10)\eta}{256(s-1)}(k+s)^{2}\|\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}.\end{array}$
Hence, utilizing this inequality, with similar arguments as the proof of the first bound of (33), we obtain the second line of (33) from this expression.
Step 4 (Other intermediate summability results).
Next, rearranging (80), we have
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}\big]&\leq&\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\big]-\frac{\eta s(k+s+1)}{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}\big]-\frac{(s-2)(4s-7)\eta(k+1)}{64(s-1)}\mathbb{E}\big[\|w^{k}\|^{2}\big]\\
&&-{~}\frac{(s-2)\eta L^{2}t_{k}^{2}}{32(s-1)}\mathbb{E}\big[\|x^{k+1}-y^{k}\|^{2}\big]-\big[\varphi_{s}+\frac{25s-34}{2(s-2)}\big]\epsilon_{2}\eta t_{k-1}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\Delta_{k-1}\big]-\frac{2\alpha\rho_{c}t_{k}^{2}}{1-\alpha}\mathbb{E}\big[\|w^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}\big]\\
&&-{~}\frac{\eta t_{k}(t_{k}-s)}{2}\big(1-\frac{M^{2}\eta^{2}t_{k}}{t_{k}-s}\big)\mathbb{E}\big[\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-w^{k}\|^{2}\big]+\big[\varphi_{s}+\frac{25s-34}{2(s-2)}\big]\frac{\eta\delta_{k}}{\bar{c}}.\end{array}$
Since $\epsilon_{2}>0$ and $\eta<{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{\hat{\lambda}}{L}}$ as in (31), the terms from the second to seventh on the right-hand side of this inequality are all nonnegative.
Under the assumption $\mathcal{S}_{\infty}:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\delta_{k}<+\infty$ , we can apply Lemma A.1 to the last inequality and obtain that
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|x^{k+1}-y^{k}\|^{2}\big]&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}(t_{k}-s)\mathbb{E}\big[\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-w^{k}\|^{2}\big]&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\Delta_{k}\big]&<&+\infty.\end{array}$
(83)
From (3) of Assumption 1, for any $\alpha\in[0,1)$ , we have $|\!\|w^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k+1}|\!\|^{2}\leq\frac{L^{2}}{1-\alpha}\|x^{k+1}-y^{k}\|^{2}$ .
Using this bound, the relations $|\!\|\cdot|\!\|\geq\|\cdot\|$ , and $\mathbb{E}\big[\|e^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\Delta_{k}\big]$ from the first line of (27) after taking total expectations, we also obtain from (83) that
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[|\!\|w^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k+1}|\!\|^{2}\big]&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|w^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}\big]&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|e^{k}\|^{2}\big]&<&+\infty.\end{array}$
(84)
By Young’s inequality, we easily get
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\|w^{k+1}-w^{k}\|^{2}&\leq&2\|w^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}+2\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-w^{k}\|^{2},\\
\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}&\leq&2\|w^{k}-\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}+2\|w^{k}-\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}.\end{array}$
Moreover, we also have $\mathbb{E}\big[\|\hat{e}^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq 2\mathbb{E}\big[\|e^{k-1}\|^{2}\big]+2\mathbb{E}\big[\|w^{k}-\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}\big]$ .
Using these relations, we can obviously derive from (83) and (84) that
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|w^{k+1}-w^{k}\|^{2}\big]&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k}\|^{2}\big]&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}t_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|\hat{e}^{k}\|^{2}\big]&<&+\infty.\end{array}$
(85)
Step 5 (Applying Lemmas 5.6 and 5.7).
The proof of (36) follows the same arguments as the proof of Step 4 in Theorem 3.1 and we omit the details.
The iteration-complexity stated in Statement (d) is also proven as in Theorem 3.1, and we omit it here.
Step 6 (Almost sure convergence).
The almost sure convergence results in Statement (e) follow from an argument similar to the proof of Theorem 3.2, and hence we omit the details.
$\square$
6 Numerical Experiments.
In this section, we present three numerical examples to validate our algorithm, (VAPEG), and compare it with closely related methods.
Our Python implementation was run on an Apple M4 MacBook Pro (24 GB RAM, 1 TB SSD).
6.1 Bilinear matrix game: Policeman vs. Burglar problem.
Consider a city with $m^{2}$ houses distributed on a grid $m\times m$ , where the $j$ -th house has wealth $w_{j}$ for all $j=1,\cdots,m^{2}$ .
Each night, a Burglar selects a house $j$ to rob, while a Policeman chooses a location to station himself near house $k$ .
Once the burglary begins, the Policeman is immediately informed of the burglary location, and the probability that he apprehends the Burglar is given by $\mathbf{p}_{c}:=\exp\{-\theta\,\mathrm{dist}(j,k)\}$ , where $\mathrm{dist}(j,k)$ is the distance between houses $j$ and $k$ .
However, neither the Burglar nor the Policeman has access to the true wealth $w_{j}$ ; instead, they need to estimate it using a set of $n$ observations ${\hat{w}^{(i)}_{j}}$ for $i\in[n]$ .
The Burglar aims to maximize his expected reward, which is $\mathcal{R}_{jk}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\hat{w}^{(i)}_{j}}\left(1-\exp\{-\theta\,\mathrm{dist}(j,k)\}\right)$ ,
while the Policeman’s objective is to minimize $\mathcal{R}_{jk}$ .
$\mathrm{(a)}$ Mathematical model.
The Policeman vs. Burglar problem described above can be viewed as a zero-sum game.
It was studied in [80] and we slightly modify it to obtain a stochastic version.
Following [80], we define a payoff matrix $\mathbf{L}$ as follows:
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbf{L}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{L}^{(i)},\quad\mathbf{L}^{(i)}_{j,k}={\hat{w}^{(i)}_{j}}\left(1-\exp\{-\theta\,\mathrm{dist}(j,k)\}\right),\quad 1\leq j,k\leq m^{2}.\end{array}$
Then, we can formulate the above problem as the following classical bilinear matrix game:
$\min_{u\in\Delta_{p_{1}}}\max_{v\in\Delta_{p_{2}}}\Big\{\mathcal{L}(u,v):=\langle\mathbf{L}u,v\rangle\Big\},$
(86)
where $u$ and $v$ represent mixed strategies of the Policeman and Burglar, and $\Delta_{p_{1}}$ and $\Delta_{p_{2}}$ are the standard simplexes in $\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}}$ and $\mathbb{R}^{p_{2}}$ , respectively with $p_{1}=p_{2}=m^{2}$ .
Let us define $x:=[u;v]\in\mathbb{R}^{p}$ with $p:=p_{1}+p_{2}$ , $G_{i}x:=\mathbf{G}_{i}x=[\mathbf{L}^{(i)\top}v;-\mathbf{L}^{(i)}u]$ for $i\in[n]$ , and $Tx:=[\partial\delta_{\Delta_{p_{1}}}(u);\partial\delta_{\Delta_{p_{2}}}(v)]$ , where $\delta_{\mathcal{X}}(\cdot)$ is the indicator of $\mathcal{X}$ .
Then, we can write the optimality condition of (86) as $0\in\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}G_{i}x+Tx$ , a special case of (GE).
Since $\mathbf{G}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{G}_{i}$ is a skew-symmetric matrix, $G$ is monotone and $L$ -Lipschitz continuous.
$\mathrm{(b)}$ Input data.
Following [80], we first choose $\mathrm{dist}(j,k):=|j-k|$ and $\theta:=0.8$ .
Next, we generate the nominal wealth $w_{j}$ from a standard normal distribution, and then take $|w_{j}|$ to ensure its nonnegativity.
Then, the noisy wealth $w^{(i)}_{j}$ is generated as $w^{(i)}_{j}:=|w_{j}+\epsilon^{(i)}_{j}|$ , where $\epsilon^{(i)}_{j}$ is a normal random variable with zero mean and variance $\sigma^{2}=0.05$ .
Finally, we produce two sets of experiments, each containing $10$ problem instances, as follows.
•
Experiment 1. $m=10$ and $n=1000$ , corresponding to $p_{1}=100$ and $p=2p_{1}=200$ .
•
Experiment 2. $m=15$ and $n=2000$ , corresponding to $p_{1}=225$ and $p=2p_{1}=450$ .
$\mathrm{(c)}$ Algorithms and parameters.
We choose three competitors as follows.
•
The deterministic optimistic gradient method, e.g., in [32], abbreviated by OG.
We consider this method as a baseline for our comparison.
•
The variance-reduced forward-reflected-backward splitting algorithm in [2], called VrFRBS.
•
The variance-reduced extragradient (EG) algorithm in [1], abbreviated by VrEG.
Both VrFRBS and VrEG are variants of the EG method and use a Loopless SVRG estimator.
We implement four variants of our method (VAPEG): VAPEG-Sgd, VAPEG-Svrg, VAPEG-Saga, and VAPEG-Sarah, and compare them with OG, VrFRBS, and VrEG.
Here, VAPEG-Sgd uses the mini-batch estimator (16), while the last three variants use L-SVRG, SAGA, and L-SARAH estimators, respectively.
We run all algorithms for 200 epochs, where each epoch corresponds to $n$ calls of $\mathbf{G}$ .
The parameters of these algorithms are chosen as follows.
For all four variants of our methods, we choose $\eta=\frac{1}{8L}$ , (i.e., $s=3$ and $\omega=7$ ).
For OG, we choose $\eta=\frac{1}{L}$ .
For VrFRBS, we choose $\eta=\frac{0.95(1-\sqrt{1-\mathbf{p}})}{2L}$ , and for VrEG, we choose $\eta=\frac{0.95\sqrt{1-\alpha}}{L}$ with $\alpha:=1-\mathbf{p}$ .
For the probability of reference points and mini-batch size, we choose:
•
$\mathbf{p}=0.5n^{-1/3}$ for VAPEG-Svrg, VrFRBS, and VrEG.
For example, if $n=1000$ , then $\mathbf{p}=0.05$ , and if $n=2000$ , then $\mathbf{p}=0.04$ .
•
$\mathbf{p}=0.5n^{-1/2}$ for VAPEG-Sarah.
If $n=1000$ , then $\mathbf{p}=0.016$ , while if $n=2000$ , then $\mathbf{p}=0.011$ .
•
$b_{k}=\lfloor 0.5n^{2/3}\rfloor$ for VAPEG-Svrg, VAPEG-Saga, VrFRBS, and VrEG.
In this case, if $n=1000$ , then $b_{k}=50$ , and if $n=2000$ , then $b_{k}=79$ .
•
$b_{k}=\lfloor 0.5n^{1/2}\rfloor$ for VAPEG-Sarah (e.g., $b_{k}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}15}$ for $n=1000$ and $b_{k}=22$ for $n=2000$ ).
•
For VAPEG-Sgd, we choose increasing mini-batch size $b_{k}=\max\{5,\min\{0.05(l+1)^{3},n\}\}$ , where $l$ is the epoch counter.
This choice makes $b_{k}$ increasing, but not too fast since $b_{k}=\mathcal{O}\big((k+s)^{3}\big)$ .
The initial point is chosen as $x^{0}:=[\frac{1}{p_{1}}\cdot\texttt{ones($p_{1}$)};\frac{1}{p_{2}}\cdot\texttt{ones($p_{2}$)}]$ for all methods.
$\mathrm{(d)}$ Numerical results.
The results of these experiments are presented in Figure 2.
Figure 2:
The performance of the seven algorithms for solving the bilinear matrix game (86) in two experiments, averaging over $10$ problem instances each.
Here, $\mathcal{G}_{\eta}$ is the FBS residual defined by (26).
From Figure 2, we can see that with the theoretical parameters, two VAPEG variants: VAPEG-Saga and VAPEG-Sarah significantly outperform the competitors in both experiments.
However, VAPEG-Saga is slightly better than VAPEG-Sarah since VAPEG-Saga uses only one batch $\mathcal{B}_{k}$ as opposed to two in VAPEG-Sarah per iteration.
As a compensation, VAPEG-Saga requires storing a table of $n$ operator values.
Our VAPEG-Svrg also works well, but it is worse than VAPEG-Saga and VAPEG-Sarah.
With its theoretical parameters, VrFRBS exhibits the poorest performance among the tested methods.
VrEG is still worse than the baseline scheme OG.
Our VAPEG-Sgd performs well and better than other methods in early epochs, but then it plateaus at a certain accuracy level, perhaps due to the effect of variance.
Next, we test the effect of the probability $\mathbf{p}_{k}$ and the mini-batch size $b_{k}$ of $\mathcal{B}_{k}$ using the same experiments as in Figure 2.
For the methods involving a reference-refresh probability, we halve $p_{k}$ , and for the applicable control-variate methods we also halve $b_{k}$ ; the VAPEG-Sgd batch schedule is left unchanged.
Then, the results are presented in Figure 3.
Figure 3: The performance of seven algorithms with a smaller probability $\mathbf{p}_{k}$ and mini-batch size $b_{k}$ to solve the bilinear matrix game (86) in two experiments, averaging over $10$ problem instances each.
The performance of control-variate methods improves because of the increased number of inner iterations per epoch.
This improvement is seen across all variants and consistent with the first test.
6.2 Markov decision processes.
Markov decision processes (MDPs) are commonly used to model sequential decision-making problems [91], with applications in numerous areas such as reinforcement learning [78] and healthcare [3, 47].
$\mathrm{(a)}$ Mathematical model.
A finite MDP consists of a state space $\mathcal{S}:=\{1,2,\cdots,n\}$ with $n$ states and a finite action space $\mathcal{A}:=\{1,2,\cdots,m\}$ with $m$ actions.
Each state-action pair $(s,a)$ is associated with a reward $r_{sa}$ and a transition probability distribution $P_{sa}\in\Delta_{n}$ over the next states.
Define $r_{\infty}:=\max_{s,a}r_{sa}$ .
Without loss of generality, we assume $r_{sa}\geq 0$ for all $(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}$ .
Given a discount factor $\bar{\gamma}\in(0,1)$ and an initial state distribution $\mathbf{p}_{0}\in\Delta_{n}$ , a common objective in MDPs is to maximize the expected infinite-horizon discounted reward.
Following the same procedure as in [53, 91], this MDP problem can be reformulated into the following convex-concave saddle-point problem:
${\min_{v\geq 0,\|v\|_{2}\leq\frac{\sqrt{n}r_{\infty}}{1-\bar{\gamma}}}\max\limits_{\mu\in\Delta_{nm}}\Big\{\mathcal{L}(v,\mu):=(1-\bar{\gamma})\mathbf{p}_{0}^{\top}v+\sum\limits_{s=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{a=1}^{m}\mu_{sa}\left(r_{sa}+\bar{\gamma}P_{sa}^{\top}v-v_{s}\right)\Big\},}\vskip-2.15277pt$
(87)
where $v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}$ is the value-function variable, whose optimal value represents the expected discounted return from each state $s\in\mathcal{S}$ , and $\mu_{sa}$ ( $s\in\mathcal{S}$ and $a\in\mathcal{A}$ ) are the dual variables.
Next, define $x:=[v;\mu]\in\mathbb{R}^{n(m+1)}$ as the concatenation of $v$ and $\mu=[\mu_{1};\cdots;\mu_{n}]\in\mathbb{R}^{nm}$ (the concatenation of $\mu_{s}$ ’s with $\mu_{s}=(\mu_{s1},\cdots,\mu_{sm})^{\top}$ , $s=1,\cdots,n$ ).
For each $s\in\mathcal{S}$ , let $B_{s}:=\left[\bar{\gamma}P_{s1}-e_{s},\cdots,\bar{\gamma}P_{sm}-e_{s}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}$ , $r_{s}:=(r_{s1},\cdots,r_{sm})^{\top}$ , $\mathcal{B}^{+}_{n}(0,R):=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}:\|v\|_{2}\leq R\}$ , and
${\begin{array}[]{lcl}G_{s}x:=\begin{bmatrix}n(1-\bar{\gamma})\mathbf{p}_{0,s}e_{s}+nB_{s}\mu_{s}\\
\mathbf{0}_{(s-1)m}\\
-nr_{s}-nB_{s}^{\top}v\\
\mathbf{0}_{(n-s)m}\end{bmatrix},\qquad Tx:=\begin{bmatrix}\partial\delta_{\mathcal{B}^{+}_{n}(0,\frac{\sqrt{n}r_{\infty}}{1-\bar{\gamma}})}(v)\\
\partial\delta_{\Delta_{nm}}(\mu)\end{bmatrix}.\end{array}}\vskip-2.15277pt$
Then, the optimality condition of (87) can be written as (GE): $0\in Gx+Tx$ , where $Gx:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{s=1}^{n}G_{s}x$ is a finite-sum representation over the state space $\mathcal{S}$ .
$\mathrm{(b)}$ Experimental setup and input data.
We evaluate the performance of (VAPEG) on solving problem (87) using randomly generated MDPs from the garnet testbed [5, 17], which is widely used for benchmarking algorithms in sequential decision-making.
Garnet MDPs are defined by a branching factor $n_{b}/n$ , representing the proportion of reachable next states from each $(s,a)$ pair.
Our setup is characterized by a tuple $(|\mathcal{S}|,|\mathcal{A}|,n_{b},\bar{\gamma})$ , and then we consider two experiments: Experiment 1 with $(|\mathcal{S}|,|\mathcal{A}|,n_{b},\bar{\gamma})=(2000,5,1000,0.9)$ , leading to $p=12000$ and Experiment 2 with $(|\mathcal{S}|,|\mathcal{A}|,n_{b},\bar{\gamma})=(4000,10,2000,0.9)$ , leading to $p=44000$ .
For each setting, we generate 10 random MDP instances where rewards are sampled uniformly at random from $[0,1]$ .
$\mathrm{(c)}$ Algorithms and parameters.
In this experiment, we use three variants of our (VAPEG): VAPEG-Svrg, VAPEG-Saga, and VAPEG-Sarah since they use control variate technique.
We again compare them with the baseline method OG, and two other competitors: VrFRBS and VrEG using SVRG estimators.
Unlike the previous example, we decide to select the parameters for all algorithms using a grid search procedure on the interval $\frac{1}{L_{B}}[10^{-5},10]$ to search for the best stepsize $\eta$ in all algorithms, where $L_{B}=\|B\|$ .
Note that $L=L_{B}$ is the Lipschitz constant of the aggregate operator $G$ .
Applying this procedure, we obtain the following values of $\eta$ for each algorithm.
•
For our variants VAPEG-Svrg, VAPEG-Saga, and VAPEG-Sarah, we find $\eta=\frac{1}{10^{3}L}$ .
•
For the baseline method: OG, we get $\eta=\frac{1}{10^{2}L}$ .
•
For VrFRBS, we obtain $\eta=\frac{0.95(1-\sqrt{1-\mathbf{p}})}{10^{2}\times 2L}$ .
•
For VrEG, we find $\eta=\frac{0.95\sqrt{1-\alpha}}{10^{3}L}$ with $\alpha:=1-\mathbf{p}$ .
As in the first example, we still choose $\mathbf{p}=0.5n^{-1/3}$ and $b=\lfloor 0.5n^{2/3}\rfloor$ for VAPEG-Svrg, VrFRBS, and VrEG, $\mathbf{p}=0.5n^{-1/2}$ and $b=\lfloor 0.5n^{1/2}\rfloor$ for VAPEG-Sarah.
The initial point is chosen as $x^{0}:=[\frac{1-\bar{\gamma}}{r_{\infty}}\texttt{ones(n)};\frac{1}{n{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}m}}\texttt{ones(n{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}m})}]$ for all methods.
$\mathrm{(d)}$ Numerical results.
We run all the algorithms using the above setting on two experiments, and the results are shown in Figure 4.
Figure 4: The performance of six algorithms to solve the infinite horizon discounted reward MDPs reformulation (87) in two experiments, averaging over 10 problem instances each.
As shown in Figure 4, the three VAPEG variants: VAPEG-Svrg, VAPEG-Saga, and VAPEG-Sarah, consistently outperform both the baseline method (OG) and competing algorithms across the entire training horizon.
Among them, VAPEG-Svrg achieves the worst residual norm, while VAPEG-Sarah demonstrates the best overall performance.
These results suggest that acceleration indeed provides a significant performance boost.
Finally, we examine the effect of the probability $\mathbf{p}_{k}$ and the mini-batch size $b_{k}$ on the performance of our methods by halving their values to obtain more inner iterations in each epoch.
The corresponding results are presented in Figure 5.
Figure 5: The performance of six algorithms to solve the infinite horizon discounted reward MDPs reformulation (87) in two experiments using smaller $\mathbf{p}$ and batch size $b$ , averaging over 10 problem instances each.
We observe that the three variants of (VAPEG) outperform their non-accelerated counterparts.
A smaller mini-batch size $b_{k}$ enables our algorithms to run more iterations, while a smaller probability $\mathbf{p}_{k}$ reduces the number of full evaluations of $G$ .
Consequently, both factors contribute to the speedup of the algorithms observed in Figure 5.
6.3 Sparse soft-robust bond portfolio construction.
In this example, we compare our algorithms with three competing methods on a sparse soft-robust bond portfolio construction problem.
The model is adapted from the robust bond portfolio formulation in [18, Section 6.2].
$\mathrm{(a)}$ Mathematical model.
Consider a portfolio of $m$ bonds with quantities $z=(z_{1},\cdots,z_{m})\in\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}$ over time periods $t=1,\cdots,T$ .
A market state is modeled by $q=(q^{u},q^{s})\in\mathbb{R}^{T+m}$ , where $q^{u}=(q^{u}_{1},\cdots,q^{u}_{T})\in\mathbb{R}^{T}$ is the yield curve and $q^{s}=(q^{s}_{1},\cdots,q^{s}_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}$ is the bond spread.
For a market state $q=(q^{u},q^{s})$ , let $\pi\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}$ denote the bond prices, where the price $\pi_{j}$ of bond $j$ is modeled by $\pi_{j}(q):=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\alpha_{j,t}\exp\big(-t(q^{u}_{t}+q^{s}_{j})\big)$ ,
with $\alpha_{j,t}\geq 0$ denoting the cash flow paid by bond $j$ at time $t$ .
Next, fix a reference market state $\bar{q}$ to evaluate local bond-price sensitivities, and let $y:=(u^{\top},s^{\top})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{T+m}$ denote changes in yields and spreads relative to this state.
Thus, the stressed state is $\bar{q}+y$ .
Since $\pi$ is highly nonlinear, we approximate it around $\bar{q}$ by $\pi(\bar{q}+y)\approx\pi(\bar{q})+J_{\pi}(\bar{q})y$ , where $J_{\pi}$ is the Jacobian of the bond-price map at $\bar{q}$ .
Denoting $B:=-J_{\pi}(\bar{q})$ , we obtain $\pi(\bar{q}+y)^{\top}z\approx\pi(\bar{q})^{\top}z-z^{\top}By$ .
Let $\phi:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}$ be a smooth convex nominal portfolio objective, which may include tracking error against a benchmark, risk, or transaction cost terms.
Absorbing the reference-state portfolio value, we define $f(z):=\phi(z)-\pi(\bar{q})^{\top}z$ , which remains smooth and convex.
Now, instead of using a bounded uncertainty set together with a nonsmooth uncertainty penalty, we penalize the overall magnitude of the market shock and its movements along prescribed market-shock patterns through
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}h(y)&:=&\frac{\gamma}{2}\|y\|^{2}+\nu\sum_{\ell=1}^{r}\log\cosh(c_{\ell}^{\top}y),\end{array}$
(88)
where $\gamma>0$ , $\nu>0$ , and $C\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times(T+m)}$ has rows $c_{\ell}^{\top}$ , $\ell\in[r]$ .
Each vector $c_{\ell}$ represents a prescribed pattern of joint movements in yields and spreads, while $c_{\ell}^{\top}y$ measures the signed projection of the market shock onto that pattern.
Since $\log\cosh(\cdot)$ is even and increasing in the absolute value of its argument, the second term penalizes large movements along these patterns.
The parameter $\gamma$ controls the overall magnitude penalty, while $\nu$ controls the strength of the pattern-specific penalty.
Finally, to promote a sparse bond portfolio, we use the SCAD penalty $\mathcal{R}_{\lambda,\theta}(z):=\sum_{j=1}^{m}R_{\lambda,\theta}(z_{j})$ proposed in [42] as a continuous surrogate for cardinality.
The resulting sparse soft-robust bond portfolio model is the following nonconvex-concave minimax problem:
$\min_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\max_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{T+m}}\left\{\mathcal{L}(z,y):=f(z)+z^{\top}By-h(y)+\tau\mathcal{R}_{\lambda,\theta}(z)\right\}$
(89)
Here, $\lambda>0$ determines the SCAD threshold, $\theta>2$ is the SCAD shape parameter, and $\tau>0$ controls the strength of the sparsity regularization.
The SCAD penalty can be decomposed as $R_{\lambda,\theta}(t)=\lambda|t|-\tilde{R}_{\lambda,\theta}(t)$ , where
$\tilde{R}_{\lambda,\theta}(t)=\begin{cases}0,&|t|\leq\lambda,\\[2.84526pt]
\dfrac{(|t|-\lambda)^{2}}{2(\theta-1)},&\lambda<|t|\leq\theta\lambda,\\[5.69054pt]
\lambda|t|-\dfrac{(\theta+1)\lambda^{2}}{2},&|t|>\theta\lambda.\end{cases}$
(90)
Let $\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\lambda,\theta}(z):=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tilde{R}_{\lambda,\theta}(z_{j})$ .
Then, the saddle-point problem (89) can be written equivalently as
$\min_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{m}}\max_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{T+m}}\left\{\mathcal{L}(z,y):=f(z)+z^{\top}By-h(y)-\tau\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\lambda,\theta}(z)+\tau\lambda\|z\|_{1}+\delta_{\mathcal{Z}}(z)\right\},$
(91)
where $\delta_{\mathcal{Z}}$ is the indicator function of the closed convex set $\mathcal{Z}$ .
Suppose $f(z)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(z)$ ,
where every $f_{i}$ is continuously differentiable.
Now, let us define
$x=\begin{bmatrix}z\\
y\end{bmatrix},\qquad G_{i}x:=\begin{bmatrix}\nabla f_{i}(z)+By-\tau\nabla\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\lambda,\theta}(z)\\
\nabla h(y)-B^{\top}z\end{bmatrix},\quad\text{and}\quad Tx:=\begin{bmatrix}\tau\lambda\partial\|z\|_{1}+\partial\delta_{\mathcal{Z}}(z)\\
0\end{bmatrix}.$
(92)
Then, the optimality condition of (91) can be written as $0\in Gx+Tx$ , where $Gx:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}G_{i}x$ .
Proposition 6.1 summarizes the properties of $G$ and $T$ , with its proof deferred to Appendix C.6.
Proposition 6.1
Suppose that $f$ in (91) is convex and $L_{f}$ -average smooth, i.e.:
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\nabla f_{i}(z)-\nabla f_{i}(z^{\prime})\|^{2}\leq L_{f}^{2}\|z-z^{\prime}\|^{2},\qquad\forall z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.\end{array}$
Suppose further that $B\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times(T+m)}$ in (91) has full row rank such that its smallest singular value $\sigma:=\sigma_{\min}(B^{\top})>L_{h}\big(\frac{\tau}{\gamma(\theta-1)}\big)^{1/2}$ , where $L_{h}:=\gamma+\nu\|C\|^{2}$ .
Then the following statements hold:
$\mathrm{(i)}$
The mapping $G$ in (92) is $L$ -Lipschitz continuous with $L:=\frac{1}{2}\big[\tilde{L}_{f}+L_{h}+\big[(\tilde{L}_{f}-L_{h})^{2}+4\|B\|^{2}\big]^{1/2}\big]$ , where $\tilde{L}_{f}:=L_{f}+\frac{\tau}{\theta-1}$ .
$\mathrm{(ii)}$
The mapping $\Phi:=G+T$ is globally $\rho$ -co-hypomonotone, with $\rho:=\frac{\tau}{(\theta-1)\big(\sigma-L_{h}\sqrt{\tau/((\theta-1)\gamma)}\big)^{2}}$ .
$\mathrm{(iii)}$
The mapping $\Phi:=G+T$ satisfies Assumption 1 with $\rho_{n}:=(1+\kappa)\rho$ and $\rho_{c}:=\frac{\kappa\rho}{1+\hat{L}^{2}}$ , where $\hat{L}^{2}:=\frac{L^{2}\big\{\gamma^{2}L_{h}^{2}(\theta-1)^{2}\left(\gamma^{2}+\|B\|^{2}\right)+\big[\gamma\left(\sigma^{2}(\theta-1)-\tau L_{h}\right)+L_{h}(\theta-1)\|B\|\sqrt{\gamma^{2}+\|B\|^{2}}\big]^{2}\big\}}{\gamma^{4}[\sigma^{2}(\theta-1)-\tau L_{h}]^{2}}$ and $\kappa>0$ arbitrarily.
$\mathrm{(b)}$ Data generation.
This example aims to illustrate the performance of our methods and their competitors on a nonmonotone problem.
The data is generated synthetically as follows.
$\mathrm{(i)}$ Bond cash flows and reference market state.
For each bond $j\in[m]$ , we independently generate its maturity and coupon rate according to
$M_{j}\sim\operatorname{Unif}\{2,\cdots,T\}$ and $\kappa_{j}\sim\operatorname{Unif}[0.018,0.042]$ , respectively.
The face value is normalized to one, and the cash flows are
$\alpha_{j,t}:=\kappa_{j}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{t\leq M_{j}\}}+\boldsymbol{1}_{\{t=M_{j}\}}$ .
The reference yield curve is deterministic, $\bar{q}_{t}^{u}:=0.018+\frac{0.012}{T-1}(t-1)$ , $t\in[T]$ , while the bond-specific reference spreads are independently generated from $\bar{q}_{j}^{s}\sim\operatorname{Unif}[0.004,0.007]$ , $j\in[m]$ .
Once the reference market state $\bar{q}$ is generated, the matrix $B=-J_{\pi}(\bar{q})\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times(T+m)}$ is constructed analytically. Specifically, the yield-curve and spread blocks of $B$ are given by
$\begin{array}[]{lcll}B_{j,t}&=&t\alpha_{j,t}\exp\bigl(-t(\bar{q}_{t}^{u}+\bar{q}_{j}^{s})\bigr),&\qquad t\in[T],\\
B_{j,T+\ell}&=&\mathbf{1}_{\{j=\ell\}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}t\alpha_{j,t}\exp\bigl(-t(\bar{q}_{t}^{u}+\bar{q}_{j}^{s})\bigr),&\qquad\ell\in[m].\end{array}$
Since the spread block is diagonal with strictly positive diagonal entries, every generated matrix $B$ has full row rank, and thus $\sigma:=\sigma_{\min}(B^{\top})>0$ .
$\mathrm{(ii)}$ Market-stress directions.
For each setting, define $\boldsymbol{t}:=(1,2,\cdots,T)^{\top}$ , $\boldsymbol{1}_{T}:=(1,\cdots,1)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{T}$ , $\bar{t}:=\frac{T+1}{2}$ , and
$\widetilde{\boldsymbol{t}}:=\boldsymbol{t}-\bar{t}\,\boldsymbol{1}_{T}$ .
The level, slope, and curvature directions and the common credit-spread direction are defined as
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}c^{\mathrm{lev}}:=\frac{\boldsymbol{1}_{T}}{\sqrt{T}},\qquad c^{\mathrm{slp}}:=\frac{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{t}}}{\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{t}}\|},\qquad c^{\mathrm{curv}}:=\frac{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{t}}^{\odot 2}-\frac{\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{t}}\|^{2}}{T}\boldsymbol{1}_{T}}{\big\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{t}}^{\odot 2}-\frac{\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{t}}\|^{2}}{T}\boldsymbol{1}_{T}\big\|},\qquad\text{and}\qquad c^{\mathrm{spr}}:=\frac{\boldsymbol{1}_{m}}{\sqrt{m}},\end{array}$
respectively, where ⊙2 denotes the elementwise square.
We then set
$C:=\begin{bmatrix}(c^{\mathrm{lev}})^{\top}&0\\
(c^{\mathrm{slp}})^{\top}&0\\
(c^{\mathrm{curv}})^{\top}&0\\
0&(c^{\mathrm{spr}})^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{4\times(T+m)}.$
The four rows of $C$ are orthonormal, and hence $\|C\|=1$ .
We use $\nu=1$ and $\gamma:=\nu\|C\|^{2}=1$ .
Thus, $L_{h}=\gamma+\nu\|C\|^{2}=2$ .
$\mathrm{(iii)}$ Finite-sum scenarios.
The shifted portfolio objective is chosen as $f(z):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(z)$ , where $f_{i}(z):=\frac{1}{2}(g_{i}^{\top}z-r_{i})^{2}+\frac{\mu}{2}\|z\|^{2}+b^{\top}z$ .
Here, $g_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}$ represents the modeled bond responses in scenario $i$ , so that $g_{i}^{\top}z$ is the aggregate portfolio response, while $r_{i}\in\mathbb{R}$ is the corresponding benchmark response.
Thus, the first term is the average squared tracking error, while the remaining terms provide quadratic regularization and a linear portfolio adjustment.
Suppose that each market scenario is characterized by a $6$ -dimensional factor model.
We first define the standardized bond features
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\widehat{M}_{j}:=\frac{M_{j}-\bar{M}}{\mathrm{sd}_{M}},\qquad\widehat{\kappa}_{j}:=\frac{\kappa_{j}-\bar{\kappa}}{\mathrm{sd}_{\kappa}},\qquad\widehat{s}_{j}:=\frac{\bar{q}_{j}^{s}-\bar{s}}{\mathrm{sd}_{s}},\end{array}$
where $(\bar{M},\mathrm{sd}_{M})$ , $(\bar{\kappa},\mathrm{sd}_{\kappa})$ , and $(\bar{s},\mathrm{sd}_{s})$ are the empirical mean and standard deviation of maturity, coupon, and spread, respectively.
We form
$\widehat{F}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times 6}$ row-wise as
$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\widehat{F}_{j,:}:=\big[1,\ \widehat{M}_{j},\ \widehat{M}_{j}^{2}-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\widehat{M}_{\ell}^{2},\ \widehat{\kappa}_{j},\ \widehat{s}_{j},\ \widehat{M}_{j}\widehat{s}_{j}\big].\end{array}$
The six columns represent a common movement, linear and quadratic maturity effects, a coupon effect, a credit-spread effect, and a maturity–credit interaction.
Let $D_{F}:=\operatorname{diag}\bigl(\|\widehat{F}_{:,1}\|,\cdots,\|\widehat{F}_{:,6}\|\bigr)$ .
If any empirical standard deviation or column norm of $\widehat{F}$ is zero, we discard the draw and resample the corresponding bond characteristics, ensuring that $D_{F}$ is nonsingular.
We then define $F:=a_{F}\widehat{F}D_{F}^{-1}$ so that all factors have equal magnitude $a_{F}$ .
For every $i\in[n]$ , we independently generate $\omega_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{6})$ and set $g_{i}:=F\omega_{i}$ .
To construct a low-curvature direction and verify nonmonotonicity, we impose two bonds with identical observable characteristics.
This imposition is applied before constructing the cash flows, $B$ , $F$ , and the scenario vectors $g_{i}$ .
For each problem instance, we select
$j_{1}:=\left\lfloor\frac{m}{4}\right\rfloor$ , $j_{2}:=j_{1}+1$ , and set $M_{j_{2}}:=M_{j_{1}}$ , $\kappa_{j_{2}}:=\kappa_{j_{1}}$ , and $\bar{q}_{j_{2}}^{s}:=\bar{q}_{j_{1}}^{s}$ .
It follows that $F_{j_{2},:}=F_{j_{1},:}$ and hence $g_{i,j_{1}}=g_{i,j_{2}}$ for every $i$ .
Therefore, defining $d_{\mathrm{sw}}:=\frac{e_{j_{1}}-e_{j_{2}}}{\sqrt{2}}$ , where $e_{j}$ is the $j^{\mathrm{th}}$ -unit vector, we have $g_{i}^{\top}d_{\mathrm{sw}}=0$ for every $i$ .
$\mathrm{(iv)}$ Selection of the theoretical parameters.
We use the same SCAD and box-constraint parameters for all instances: $\mathcal{Z}=[0,1]^{m}$ , $\lambda=0.25$ , and $\theta=2.5$ .
The parameters $\tau$ and $\mu$ are selected as follows.
For each instance, we initialize $\tau:=(\theta-1)\min\{0.05,0.1\gamma\big(\frac{\sigma}{L_{h}}\big)^{2}\}$ and set $\mu:=\frac{\tau}{4(\theta-1)}$ .
This choice satisfies $\sigma^{2}>L_{h}^{2}\frac{\tau}{(\theta-1)\gamma}$ .
Next, we compute the average smoothness parameter of $f$ as $L_{f}:=\big[\lambda_{\max}\big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(g_{i}g_{i}^{\top}+\mu\mathbb{I})^{2}\big)\big]^{1/2}$ .
If we denote $\tilde{L}_{f}:=L_{f}+\frac{\tau}{\theta-1}$ , then the Lipschitz constant of $G$ as $L=\frac{\tilde{L}_{f}+L_{h}+\sqrt{(\tilde{L}_{f}-L_{h})^{2}+4\|B\|^{2}}}{2}$ .
